\chapter{Packages and
Reproducibility}\label{packages-and-reproducibility}

\section{\texorpdfstring{R \texttt{library()} vs.~Python
\texttt{import}}{R library() vs.~Python import}}\label{r-library-vs.-python-import}

Both languages require you to load packages before using them.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
R
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Python
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Purpose
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{library(readr)} & \texttt{import\ pandas\ as\ pd} & read CSV
files \\
\texttt{library(dplyr)} & \texttt{import\ pandas\ as\ pd} & filter,
group, and summarise data \\
\texttt{library(ggplot2)} & \texttt{import\ seaborn\ as\ sns} & create
plots \\
\texttt{library(reticulate)} & not applicable & call Python from R \\
\end{longtable}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Why \texttt{requirements.txt}
Matters}{Why requirements.txt Matters}}\label{why-requirements.txt-matters}

Python projects record package dependencies in
\texttt{requirements.txt}. This is the Python-side equivalent of saying,
``Here is what another person must install before my code will run.''

The course Codespace already provides \texttt{pandas} and the notebook
tools, so you should not need to install anything for this week's
required work. Only edit \texttt{.devcontainer/requirements.txt} if an
additional Python package has been approved.

In the course Codespace this file lives at
\texttt{.devcontainer/requirements.txt} (at the top level of your
project). The file itself is simple --- one package name per line. For
example, a project that depends only on \texttt{pandas} would contain:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{pandas}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you also create a plot in Python with packages that are not
preinstalled, add:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{seaborn}
\NormalTok{matplotlib}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Course Rule}\label{course-rule}

If you rely on an approved additional Python package, record it in the
root \texttt{.devcontainer/requirements.txt} and in your dependency
note. Do not create a second submission-folder requirements file.

\section{R Connection}\label{r-connection}

R reproducibility uses the same devcontainer baseline plus explicit
\texttt{library()} calls and dependency notes. Week 5 is a bridge: you
should be able to explain where dependencies are recorded in both
languages.

\chapter{Translation Traps}\label{translation-traps}

AI translation is useful, but it often hides small language differences.
These are the traps to check first.

\section{Trap 1: Indexing}\label{trap-1-indexing}

R starts counting at 1. Python starts counting at 0.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{values }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{values[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{values }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{values[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Python slices are end-exclusive:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{values[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This returns the first two values, not the first three.

Walk through it position by position. Python labels the items position 0
(\texttt{10}), position 1 (\texttt{20}), and position 2 (\texttt{30}).
The slice \texttt{values{[}0:2{]}} starts at position 0 and stops
\emph{just before} position 2 --- that is what ``end exclusive'' means:
the end position itself is left out. So you get \texttt{10} and
\texttt{20}, but not \texttt{30}. The matching R code is
\texttt{values{[}1:2{]}}, because R starts counting at 1 and includes
both ends. This off-by-one difference is one of the most common AI
translation bugs, so always count a small example by hand.

\section{Trap 2: Indentation}\label{trap-2-indentation}

R uses braces to mark code blocks. Python uses indentation.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{) \{}
  \StringTok{"adult"}
\NormalTok{\} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
  \StringTok{"not in adult cohort"}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ age }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{:}
    \CommentTok{"adult"}
\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
    \CommentTok{"not in adult cohort"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If Python indentation is inconsistent, the code may fail or run with a
different meaning.

\section{Trap 3: Assignment
vs.~Comparison}\label{trap-3-assignment-vs.-comparison}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Meaning & R & Python \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
assign a value & \texttt{x\ \textless{}-\ 10} & \texttt{x\ =\ 10} \\
compare values & \texttt{x\ ==\ 10} & \texttt{x\ ==\ 10} \\
missing/null check & \texttt{is.na(x)} & \texttt{pd.isna(x)} or
\texttt{x\ is\ None} \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Trap 4: Missing Values}\label{trap-4-missing-values}

R and Python both have missing values, but they are handled by different
functions. Always check missingness before comparing summaries.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(nhanes}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{BMI))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{nhanes[}\StringTok{"BMI"}\NormalTok{].isna().}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Audit Habit}\label{audit-habit}

When a translated result differs, check indexing, indentation,
assignment/comparison, missingness handling, and grouping variables
before assuming one language is ``wrong.''

\section{Compare To R}\label{compare-to-r}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Question

R

\chapter{Python Tracebacks}\label{python-tracebacks}

\section{The Bottom Line Is Usually At The
Bottom}\label{the-bottom-line-is-usually-at-the-bottom}

Python errors often print several lines called a traceback. Read from
the bottom upward:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{FileNotFoundError: [Errno 2] No such file or directory: \textquotesingle{}Data/patients.csv\textquotesingle{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This tells you the immediate problem: Python cannot find the file at the
path you gave it.

\section{Common Week 5 Errors}\label{common-week-5-errors}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Error
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Likely cause
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
First fix
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{FileNotFoundError} & path is wrong, file is in another folder,
or capitalization differs & check the relative path from the notebook
location \\
\texttt{ModuleNotFoundError} & package is missing & add the package to
\texttt{requirements.txt} and install it in the Codespace \\
\texttt{KeyError} & column name is misspelled or not present & run
\texttt{nhanes.columns.tolist()} \\
\texttt{IndentationError} & Python block spacing is inconsistent &
re-indent the block with four spaces \\
\texttt{NameError} & object has not been created in the current kernel &
rerun earlier cells in order \\
\end{longtable}

\section{AI Debugging Prompt}\label{ai-debugging-prompt}

\begin{quote}
Read this Python traceback from the bottom up. Identify the error type,
the exact object or path causing the issue, and the smallest code change
that would fix it. Do not rewrite the full analysis.
\end{quote}

\section{Student Check}\label{student-check-1}

When you fix an error, add one sentence to your notebook explaining what
failed and how you knew the fix worked.

\chapter{One Step, One Cell}\label{one-step-one-cell}

\section{Why This Rule Exists}\label{why-this-rule-exists}

Large AI-generated code blocks are hard to audit. A single hidden
mistake in loading, filtering, grouping, or missingness handling can
cascade into a polished but incorrect result.

Use one cell per analytic step:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  load data;
\item
  inspect rows and columns;
\item
  filter missing values;
\item
  group and summarise;
\item
  compare with the R result.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Good AI Prompt}\label{good-ai-prompt}

\begin{quote}
Translate only the data-loading step from R to Python/pandas. Tell me
what output I should inspect before moving to the next step.
\end{quote}

\section{Risky AI Prompt}\label{risky-ai-prompt}

\begin{quote}
Translate my full analysis, make the plots, and write the
interpretation.
\end{quote}

The risky prompt gives up too much control. It may change the analytic
question without telling you.

\section{Parity Audit Checks}\label{parity-audit-checks}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Check
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
R
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Python
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Row count & \texttt{nrow(df)} & \texttt{len(df)} or
\texttt{df.shape{[}0{]}} \\
Column names & \texttt{names(df)} & \texttt{df.columns.tolist()} \\
Unique levels & \texttt{unique(df\$IncomeGroup)} &
\texttt{df{[}"IncomeGroup"{]}.unique()} \\
Missing values & \texttt{sum(is.na(df\$BMI))} &
\texttt{df{[}"BMI"{]}.isna().sum()} \\
Summary table rows & \texttt{nrow(summary\_df)} &
\texttt{len(summary\_df)} \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Notebook Habit}\label{notebook-habit}

After each Python cell, add a short Markdown note:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{Audit check: this cell matches the R output because ...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you cannot fill in that sentence, do not move to the next cell yet.

\chapter{Mixing R and Python}\label{mixing-r-and-python}

\section{Why Interoperability
Matters}\label{why-interoperability-matters}

Sometimes a project has one useful Python tool, but the rest of the
analysis is already in R. The \texttt{reticulate} package lets R call
Python code and bring Python objects back into the R session. In this
course, \texttt{reticulate} is awareness-level: you should know what it
does and when it might be useful, but you are not expected to build a
full mixed-language pipeline.

\section{Minimal Demo}\label{minimal-demo}

Run this in an R console or an R Quarto document if \texttt{reticulate}
is available in your Codespace.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(reticulate)}

\FunctionTok{py\_run\_string}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}
\StringTok{import pandas as pd}
\StringTok{numbers = pd.DataFrame(\{\textquotesingle{}group\textquotesingle{}: [\textquotesingle{}A\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}A\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}B\textquotesingle{}], \textquotesingle{}value\textquotesingle{}: [10, 15, 20]\})}
\StringTok{summary = numbers.groupby(\textquotesingle{}group\textquotesingle{}, as\_index=False)[\textquotesingle{}value\textquotesingle{}].mean()}
\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{py}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{summary}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Health Data Example}\label{health-data-example}

The same idea works with the NHANES case-study CSV:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(reticulate)}

\FunctionTok{py\_run\_string}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}
\StringTok{import pandas as pd}
\StringTok{nhanes = pd.read\_csv(\textquotesingle{}examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv\textquotesingle{})}
\StringTok{bmi\_rows = nhanes[[\textquotesingle{}BMI\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}IncomeGroup\textquotesingle{}]].dropna()}
\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{bmi\_rows }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ py}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{bmi\_rows}
\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(bmi\_rows)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{When To Use It}\label{when-to-use-it}

Use \texttt{reticulate} when:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a small Python library solves a specific problem better than the R
  ecosystem;
\item
  the rest of your report, tables, or dashboard are already in R;
\item
  you can document the Python dependency clearly.
\end{itemize}

Avoid it when:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a clean R solution already exists;
\item
  mixing languages makes the project harder to reproduce;
\item
  the Python step cannot run in a fresh Codespace.
\end{itemize}

\section{Check Your Understanding}\label{check-your-understanding-1}

In one sentence, explain what object moves from Python back into R in
the NHANES example above.

\chapter{Reusable Python Scripts}\label{reusable-python-scripts}

\section{Notebook or Script?}\label{notebook-or-script}

Use a notebook when you are exploring, explaining, and checking outputs.
Use a script when you have a helper function that should be reused
without copying and pasting.

This mirrors the R pattern from Week 3: once a chunk of code has a clear
job, turn it into a named function.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Create
\texttt{helpers.py}}{Create helpers.py}}\label{create-helpers.py}

In your personal workspace Explorer, select
\texttt{assignments/assignment04-polyglot/submission/}. Create
\texttt{helpers.py} there, beside \texttt{translation.ipynb}; a
root-level copy will break the import below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ pathlib }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Path}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ pandas }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ pd}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ load\_and\_preview(path, n\_rows}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{):}
    \CommentTok{"""Load a CSV, print its shape, and return the first rows."""}
\NormalTok{    csv\_path }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Path(path)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ csv\_path.exists():}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{FileNotFoundError}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Could not find file: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{csv\_path}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{    data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pd.read\_csv(csv\_path)}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Loaded }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{data}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{shape[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ rows and }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{data}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{shape[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ columns"}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ data.head(n\_rows)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Import It In A Notebook}\label{import-it-in-a-notebook}

In \texttt{translation.ipynb}, import and use the helper:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ helpers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ load\_and\_preview}

\NormalTok{preview }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ load\_and\_preview(}\StringTok{"../../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{preview}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Reproducibility Check}\label{reproducibility-check-1}

Before committing:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  verify that \texttt{helpers.py} is saved in the same folder as your
  notebook;
\item
  rerun the notebook from a fresh kernel;
\item
  check that the relative path starts from the notebook location;
\item
  commit both files together with a message such as
  \texttt{Add\ Week\ 5\ Python\ helper}.
\end{itemize}

\section{Student Output}\label{student-output-1}

For Assignment 4, your reusable script should contain at least one
function that is actually called from the notebook. A function that is
written but never used does not demonstrate reproducibility.

\chapter{Git Progress}\label{git-progress}

\section{Commit The Audit Trail}\label{commit-the-audit-trail}

Week 4 introduced Git as analysis provenance. Week 5 uses that skill to
make translation auditable.

Commit after meaningful checkpoints:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  R pipeline works.
\item
  R function reproduces the pipeline.
\item
  Python notebook loads the same data.
\item
  Python summary matches the R summary.
\item
  Final parity note is complete.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Notebook Output Hygiene}\label{notebook-output-hygiene}

Jupyter notebooks can store rendered tables and images inside the
\texttt{.ipynb} file. That makes Git diffs noisy.

Before committing:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open the Command Palette with \texttt{Ctrl+Shift+P}.
\item
  Run \texttt{Notebook:\ Clear\ All\ Outputs}.
\item
  Save the notebook.
\item
  Stage \texttt{translation.ipynb}, \texttt{helpers.py}, and your Quarto
  note together.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Example Commit Messages}\label{example-commit-messages}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{Add R BMI summary for Week 5 parity check}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{Translate BMI summary to pandas and verify row counts}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{Complete Assignment 4 parity audit note}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Student Check}\label{student-check-2}

Your Git history should show the work developing in steps, not one
unexplained final upload.

\chapter{Proposal Readiness}\label{proposal-readiness}

\section{Why This Belongs In Week 5}\label{why-this-belongs-in-week-5}

Before the project proposal is due, you should know whether your chosen
dataset can be loaded, inspected, and explained in the course workflow.
Week 5 adds one extra test: can you verify the basic data shape in both
R and Python?

\section{Proposal Readiness
Checklist}\label{proposal-readiness-checklist}

Your proposal should include:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist

\item
  the dataset name and source;
\item
  a brief provenance and stewardship note;
\item
  a feasibility check showing the file can load in a Codespace;
\item
  one or two planned descriptive summaries or visualizations;
\item
  any known privacy, licensing, or access limitations.
\end{itemize}

\section{Feasibility Check}\label{feasibility-check}

For your project dataset, record:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Check & What to write \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
File path & relative path from the repo root \\
File format & CSV, RDS, Excel, API, or other \\
Row and column count & basic shape after loading \\
Key variables & names and meanings of the fields you expect to use \\
Missingness concern & one variable where missing values may matter \\
R/Python check & whether both languages can load the file \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Student Output}\label{student-output-2}

Add this feasibility check to your project proposal notes. If your
dataset does not load cleanly yet, describe the blocker honestly instead
of hiding it.

\chapter{Bilingual Translation}\label{bilingual-translation}

\section{Purpose}\label{purpose-1}

This exercise turns the week into a concrete artifact: one small R
analysis, one Python translation, and one audit note showing that the
outputs agree.

\section{Required Task}\label{required-task}

Translate the Week 5 R-deepening summary into Python/pandas:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Start from the R pipeline in \hyperref[r-deepening-and-parity-check]{R
  Deepening and Parity Check}.

  \texttt{assignments/assignment04-polyglot/submission/translation.ipynb}.

\item

\item
  Compare the Python output to the R output using row counts, grouping
  labels, and rounded summary values.
\item
  Extract one Python helper into \texttt{helpers.py} and call it from
  the notebook.
\item
  Add a Markdown cell explaining what AI helped with and how you
  verified the result.
\item
  Clear notebook outputs before committing.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Parity Table Template}\label{parity-table-template}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrrcl@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Check & R result & Python result & Match? & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Raw row count & & & & \\
Filtered row count & & & & \\
Number of grouped rows & & & & \\
Mean BMI values after rounding & & & & \\
Group labels & & & & \\
\end{longtable}

\section{AI Prompt}\label{ai-prompt}

\begin{quote}
I have a working R \texttt{dplyr} summary. Translate it to Python/pandas
one step at a time. After each step, tell me exactly what output to
compare with R. Do not add new variables, filters, plots, or
interpretation.
\end{quote}

\section{Definition of Done}\label{definition-of-done-1}

Your exercise is complete when a reader can open the notebook, rerun the
cells in order, and see why the R and Python outputs do or do not match.

\chapter{R To Python Cheatsheet}\label{r-to-python-cheatsheet}

Use this as a translation aid, not as a substitute for checking outputs.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Task
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
R
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Python
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Load package & \texttt{library(dplyr)} &
\texttt{import\ pandas\ as\ pd} \\
Read CSV & \texttt{read\_csv("file.csv")} &
\texttt{pd.read\_csv("file.csv")} \\
First rows & \texttt{head(df)} & \texttt{df.head()} \\
Row count & \texttt{nrow(df)} & \texttt{len(df)} or
\texttt{df.shape{[}0{]}} \\
Column names & \texttt{names(df)} & \texttt{df.columns.tolist()} \\
Select columns & \texttt{select(df,\ BMI,\ Gender)} &
\texttt{df{[}{[}"BMI",\ "Gender"{]}{]}} \\
Filter rows & \texttt{filter(df,\ Age\ \textgreater{}=\ 20)} &
\texttt{df{[}df{[}"Age"{]}\ \textgreater{}=\ 20{]}} \\
Missing values & \texttt{sum(is.na(df\$BMI))} &
\texttt{df{[}"BMI"{]}.isna().sum()} \\
Group summary &
\texttt{group\_by(...)\ \textbar{}\textgreater{}\ summarise(...)} &
\texttt{.groupby(...).agg(...)} \\
New column & \texttt{mutate(df,\ whtr\ =\ Waist\ /\ Height)} &
\texttt{df{[}"whtr"{]}\ =\ df{[}"Waist"{]}\ /\ df{[}"Height"{]}} \\
Sort rows & \texttt{arrange(df,\ IncomeGroup)} &
\texttt{df.sort\_values("IncomeGroup")} \\
Unique values & \texttt{unique(df\$Gender)} &
\texttt{df{[}"Gender"{]}.unique()} \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Dependency note:} \texttt{read\_csv()} comes from the
\texttt{readr} package, not \texttt{dplyr}. Load it with
\texttt{library(readr)}, or use \texttt{library(tidyverse)} to get
\texttt{readr} and \texttt{dplyr} together. On the Python side, every
command in this table needs \texttt{import\ pandas\ as\ pd} first.

\section{Translation Rule}\label{translation-rule}

The translation is not finished when the code runs. It is finished when
the R and Python outputs have been compared and the comparison is
documented.

\part{Week 6: Data Visualization}

\chapter*{Week 6: Data Visualization}\label{week-6-data-visualization-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Week 6: Data Visualization}

\markboth{Week 6: Data Visualization}{Week 6: Data Visualization}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

This week turns a descriptive summary into a visual claim. You will use
\texttt{ggplot2} as the core tool, learn how to spot the most common
ways a plot misleads a reader, practice prompting an AI co-pilot for
plot code you can defend, and finish with a polished figure for
Assignment 5.

The emphasis is not decoration. A useful plot makes the statistic,
grouping, exclusions, and limitation visible enough that a reader can
understand what the figure does and does not support.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Required / Draft / Optional This Week}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Required (graded):} the seven
  \href{../../assignments/assignment05-visualization/README.md}{Assignment
  5} files, explained one by one in
  \href{viz07-assignment-a5-and-reference.qmd}{viz07}, plus your part of
  the \href{../../milestones/m1-proposal/README.md}{M1 proposal}
  \texttt{initial-visual-plan.md}.
\item
  \textbf{Draft (studio work that feeds A5, not graded on its own):} the
  corrected plot, caption, and audit note from
  \href{viz06-studio-visual-audit.qmd}{viz06}.
\item
  \textbf{Optional (no penalty for skipping):} the alt-text practice in
  \href{viz07-assignment-a5-and-reference.qmd}{viz07}.
\end{itemize}

Tags like \texttt{{[}required{]}} and \texttt{{[}optional{]}} are
defined in \hyperref[how-to-read-submission-lists]{How To Read
Submission Lists}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Objectives}\label{objectives}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Objectives}

\markright{Objectives}

By the end of the week you can:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  build a rerunnable \texttt{ggplot2} visualization from the NHANES
  Health Equity CSV snapshot;
\item
  identify visual choices that can exaggerate, hide, or mislabel a
  descriptive result;
\item
  revise title, scale, color, grouping, and caption choices to reduce
  misinterpretation;
\item
  document whether a plot is weighted or unweighted, descriptive or
  causal, and complete-case or missingness-aware;
\item
  use AI assistance as a draft partner while checking the code and
  claims yourself.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 5 checked whether simple summaries agree across R and Python. Week
6 asks whether a visual summary is faithful to the evidence. Week 7 then
documents the cohort, missingness, and Table 1 behind those same
descriptive claims.

\section*{Case Study Data Analysis}\label{case-study-data-analysis-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Case Study Data Analysis}

\markright{Case Study Data Analysis}

The \textbf{NHANES Health Equity data spine} now supports code-reading,
EDA, visualization, and reproducibility checks. Use the cached CSV/RDS
for routine class work; a CDC retrieval script exists for advanced
users.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Cached RDS:
  \href{https://github.com/ehsanx/HDSx/blob/main/examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes_equity_v6.rds}{\texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.rds}}
\item
  CSV snapshot:
  \href{https://github.com/ehsanx/HDSx/blob/main/examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes_equity_v6.csv}{\texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}}
\item
  Case-study README:
  \href{https://github.com/ehsanx/HDSx/blob/main/examples/nhanes-equity/README.md}{\texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/README.md}}
\end{itemize}

The default classroom path is to use the cached data so the analysis
work is reproducible without internet access.

\textbf{What to do here:} you may run the code below --- it loads the
cached CSV and prints two small summaries; reading it carefully is
enough unless your week's page asks for more.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tidyverse) }\CommentTok{\# AI{-}EDIT(2026{-}06{-}23): tidyverse{-}default — consolidated core library() calls into library(tidyverse)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(nhanes\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(nhanes\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{rows =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_income =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup))}
\NormalTok{  )}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 1 x 3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}     rows missing\_bmi missing\_income}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    \textless{}int\textgreater{}       \textless{}int\textgreater{}          \textless{}int\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 1 105626        9356           9415}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup), Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 3 x 3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}   IncomeGroup            n mean\_bmi}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}   \textless{}chr\textgreater{}              \textless{}int\textgreater{}    \textless{}dbl\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 1 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5) 16330     28.6}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 2 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)  15293     29.4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 3 Middle Income      19228     29.3}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{What students leave with}\label{what-students-leave-with-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What students leave with}

\markright{What students leave with}

\chapter*{Why Visualize, and What For}\label{why-visualize-and-what-for}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Why Visualize, and What For}

\markboth{Why Visualize, and What For}{Why Visualize, and What For}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Before you write any \texttt{ggplot()} code, get clear on what a plot is
\emph{for} in a health-data project. A figure is a \textbf{claim about
evidence} --- a reader looks at it, decides what it says, and walks away
with a belief. Your job for the next two weeks is to make sure that
belief matches what the data can actually support.

\section*{The four-step pipeline you will
follow}\label{the-four-step-pipeline-you-will-follow}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The four-step pipeline you will follow}

\markright{The four-step pipeline you will follow}

This week's pages all support the same short pipeline. Every
visualization you make for the course should run through it:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Load and inspect} the data so you know what is actually there.
\item
  \textbf{Plot} a clear descriptive summary --- one figure, one
  comparison.
\item
  \textbf{Audit} the figure against the data and the question.
\item
  \textbf{Caption} the figure so a reader can interpret it without
  reading your code.
\end{enumerate}

If any step is skipped, the figure is fragile. If all four are present,
the figure can survive an AI review, a peer review, or a project grader.

\section*{What a ``good'' descriptive plot
does}\label{what-a-good-descriptive-plot-does}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What a ``good'' descriptive plot does}

\markright{What a ``good'' descriptive plot does}

A descriptive plot in this course does three things:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  It names the \textbf{statistic} (a count, mean, proportion, median).
\item
  It names the \textbf{comparison} (across what groups, time, or
  condition).
\item
  It names the \textbf{limitation} (what the figure does \emph{not}
  support --- e.g., not a causal claim, not a national estimate).
\end{itemize}

A ``bad'' plot quietly drops one of these and lets the reader fill in
the gap. Your audit habit is what catches that.

\section*{Where AI fits}\label{where-ai-fits}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where AI fits}

\markright{Where AI fits}

You will use an AI co-pilot to draft \texttt{ggplot2} code. You will not
let it choose your scale, your title, or your caption without review.
The Week 3 rule still holds: every AI-drafted chunk gets a
\texttt{\#\ AI\ prompt:\ ...\ \#\ Verified:\ ...} comment line, and you
check the column names, the missingness handling, and the claim the
figure makes.

\section*{What you produce this week}\label{what-you-produce-this-week}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What you produce this week}

\markright{What you produce this week}

Two artifacts:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  One revised, captioned \texttt{ggplot2} figure based on the NHANES
  Health Equity CSV (the Assignment 5 deliverable).
\item
  A short M1 visual plan paragraph naming the figure direction for your
  term project (one part of the Milestone 1 proposal due the same week).
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Two file formats you will keep seeing: CSV and RDS}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{CSV (\texttt{.csv})} is a plain-text table: rows and columns
  separated by commas. Any tool can open it --- R, Python, Excel, even a
  text editor. The classroom dataset \texttt{nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv} is
  a CSV.
\item
  \textbf{RDS (\texttt{.rds})} is R's own format for saving one R object
  exactly as it was. Only R reads it (with \texttt{readRDS()}). The same
  classroom dataset is also cached as \texttt{nhanes\_equity\_v6.rds}
  for R-only workflows.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{Tip}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

The plot you make for A5 does not need to be the same as the figure you
sketch for M1. A5 is practice on a known dataset; M1 is your team's plan
for your own project. The audit standard is the same in both places.

\end{tcolorbox}

\chapter*{Grammar of a ggplot, and Your First
Plot}\label{grammar-of-a-ggplot-and-your-first-plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Grammar of a ggplot, and Your First Plot}

\markboth{Grammar of a ggplot, and Your First Plot}{Grammar of a ggplot,
and Your First Plot}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

\hyperref[why-visualize-and-what-for]{viz01} framed visualization as a
claim about evidence. This page teaches the five moving parts of any
\texttt{ggplot2} figure and walks you through your first plot on the
NHANES Health Equity CSV. Everything builds from these five parts.

\section*{The five moving parts}\label{the-five-moving-parts}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The five moving parts}

\markright{The five moving parts}

Every ggplot is built from the same five ingredients. You will see all
five in the worked example on the next page, so the names matter.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Part
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
What it answers
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
A beginner-friendly analogy
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{Data} & What table are we plotting? & The contents of one box \\
\textbf{Mapping (\texttt{aes})} & Which variable goes where (x, y,
color, group)? & Telling R which column to draw on which axis \\
\textbf{Geometry (\texttt{geom\_*})} & What kind of mark (point, line,
bar)? & The shape of the ink on the page \\
\textbf{Scale and coordinates} & How are values stretched along an axis?
& The ruler under the ink \\
\textbf{Labels and theme} & What does the reader see in plain text? &
The caption around the picture \\
\end{longtable}

If a reader cannot answer ``which statistic? across what groups? with
what limitation?'' from your figure, one of these five parts is doing
too little.

\section*{Setup}\label{setup}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Setup}

\markright{Setup}

Open your Codespace and start a new code chunk in a \texttt{.qmd} file.
Run:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tidyverse)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Warning}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\texttt{library(tidyverse)} is required every session. If you see
``could not find function \texttt{ggplot}'', run the \texttt{library()}
line first.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Load the cached NHANES CSV}\label{load-the-cached-nhanes-csv}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Load the cached NHANES CSV}

\markright{Load the cached NHANES CSV}

This is the same file you will use for the worked example and your A5
deliverable. The candidate-path pattern below works whether your
\texttt{.qmd} is at the repository root or under
\texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{glimpse}\NormalTok{(nhanes)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{glimpse()} shows the column names and types. Read them
carefully. Misspelling a column name (e.g., \texttt{bmi} when the data
has \texttt{BMI}) is the \#1 source of broken ggplots in this course.

\section*{Your first plot}\label{your-first-plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Your first plot}

\markright{Your first plot}

Here is the smallest meaningful ggplot you can build from this dataset:
a scatter of one numeric variable against age, with no grouping and no
transformation. Try it as is, then change \texttt{Weight} to
\texttt{BMI} or \texttt{Height} and re-render.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(nhanes, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Age, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Weight)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha =} \FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Weight by age, NHANES Health Equity classroom dataset"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"One point per record; unweighted, descriptive only"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"Age (years)"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Weight (kg)"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Map the five parts onto this code so the grammar feels concrete:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Data:} \texttt{nhanes}
\item
  \textbf{Mapping:} \texttt{aes(x\ =\ Age,\ y\ =\ Weight)}
\item
  \textbf{Geometry:} \texttt{geom\_point(alpha\ =\ 0.2)} (translucent
  points so overlap is visible)
\item
  \textbf{Scale and coordinates:} the default linear axes (you have not
  changed them)
\item
  \textbf{Labels and theme:} \texttt{labs(...)\ +\ theme\_minimal()}
\end{itemize}

\section*{Three things to check on every
plot}\label{three-things-to-check-on-every-plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Three things to check on every plot}

\markright{Three things to check on every plot}

After you run any ggplot, ask:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Column names match the data.} Did \texttt{glimpse()} confirm
  \texttt{BMI} is uppercase, not \texttt{bmi}?
\item
  \textbf{Axes tell a clear story.} Are the units readable? Is the range
  honest?
\item
  \textbf{Title and subtitle match what is plotted.} Does the title
  overstate (``BMI rises with age across Canada'')? If yes, soften it
  (``Weight by age in the prepared classroom dataset'').
\end{enumerate}

example: load the CSV, prepare a small descriptive summary, render a

\chapter*{Worked Example: Descriptive BMI
Visualization}\label{worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Worked Example: Descriptive BMI
Visualization}

\markboth{Worked Example: Descriptive BMI Visualization}{Worked Example:
Descriptive BMI Visualization}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You learned the grammar of a ggplot and made one minimal scatter on the
\hyperref[grammar-of-a-ggplot-and-your-first-plot]{previous page}. This
page is the first end-to-end visualization of the term: load the cached
NHANES Health Equity CSV, prepare a small descriptive summary, render
one labeled figure, and write a caption that does not overclaim. We will
return to this same figure in \href{viz04-misleading-design.qmd}{viz04}
to see how easy it is to spoil with a few ``improvements.''

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

This worked example uses the NHANES Health Equity CSV snapshot to build
one descriptive visualization. The plot summarizes mean BMI by income
group across NHANES cycle. It is \textbf{unweighted} and should be
interpreted as a classroom visualization exercise, not a population
estimate.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Definition: weighted vs unweighted}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\textbf{Weighted} means the estimate accounts for survey design.
\textbf{Unweighted} means it only summarizes the observed
classroom/cache data. Use unweighted claims only as descriptive
classroom practice.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tidyverse)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(data\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Prepare a Descriptive
Summary}\label{prepare-a-descriptive-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Prepare a Descriptive Summary}

\markright{Prepare a Descriptive Summary}

Keep the summary intentionally simple. We are describing the prepared
class dataset, not estimating a national trend.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{bmi\_by\_income\_cycle }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup),}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(Cycle),}
\NormalTok{    Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(Cycle, IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{bmi\_by\_income\_cycle}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 33 x 4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    Cycle     IncomeGroup            n mean\_bmi}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    \textless{}chr\textgreater{}     \textless{}chr\textgreater{}              \textless{}int\textgreater{}    \textless{}dbl\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  1 1999{-}2000 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1123     27.9}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  2 1999{-}2000 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)   1091     28.8}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  3 1999{-}2000 Middle Income       1327     29.0}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  4 2001{-}2002 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1491     28.1}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  5 2001{-}2002 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)   1089     28.7}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  6 2001{-}2002 Middle Income       1561     28.3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  7 2003{-}2004 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1284     28.4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  8 2003{-}2004 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)   1183     28.6}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  9 2003{-}2004 Middle Income       1573     28.8}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 10 2005{-}2006 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1480     28.5}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# i 23 more rows}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Definition: hidden small cell count}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

A hidden small cell count is a group with very few observations that may
be hidden inside a chart or summary. Do not make strong claims from
these groups. That is why the code above keeps only groups with
\texttt{n\ \textgreater{}=\ 30}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Plot}\label{plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Plot}

\markright{Plot}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{bmi\_plot }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{  bmi\_by\_income\_cycle,}
  \FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Cycle, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ mean\_bmi, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup, }\AttributeTok{group =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup)}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \FloatTok{1.2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{size =} \FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{scale\_color\_brewer}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{palette =} \StringTok{"Dark2"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI by income group across NHANES cycle"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"Adults age 20{-}80 in the prepared class dataset; unweighted descriptive summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"NHANES cycle"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Income group"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{caption =} \StringTok{"Unweighted descriptive summary; not a population estimate."}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{base\_size =} \DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{axis.text.x =} \FunctionTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{angle =} \DecValTok{45}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{hjust =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"bottom"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{plot.caption =} \FunctionTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{margin =} \FunctionTok{margin}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{t =} \DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{bmi\_plot}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{weeks/week06-visualization/viz03-worked-example_files/figure-pdf/plot-bmi-by-income-cycle-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{Mean BMI is summarized descriptively from the cached class
dataset. Values are unweighted, exclude missing BMI/income/cycle values,
and should not be interpreted as causal effects or population
estimates.}

\end{figure}%

\section*{Optional Plotly Check}\label{optional-plotly-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Optional Plotly Check}

\markright{Optional Plotly Check}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Demo only --- not graded}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

This pathway is a \textbf{demonstration only}. It is not graded and not
required. Using it for graded work requires \textbf{instructor approval
first}. The supported, graded pathway in this course is the Quarto
workflow.

\end{tcolorbox}

\texttt{plotly} is an R package that turns a static ggplot into an
interactive web graphic --- you can hover over points to see values and
zoom into regions.

The core assignment uses the static \texttt{ggplot2} figure above. If
\texttt{plotly} is installed, you can preview the same plot
interactively while checking labels and tooltips. This extension is
optional because static Quarto output is the supported submission path.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\CommentTok{\# plotly::ggplotly(bmi\_plot)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Caption Template}\label{caption-template}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Caption Template}

\markright{Caption Template}

Use this structure when revising your own plot:

\begin{quote}
This figure shows {[}measure{]} by {[}group{]} across
{[}time/category{]} using the prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom
dataset. The summary is descriptive and unweighted. Records with missing
{[}variables{]} were excluded. The plot supports comparison of patterns,
not causal claims or population-level estimates.
\end{quote}

\section*{Mini Visual Audit
Checklist}\label{mini-visual-audit-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Mini Visual Audit Checklist}

\markright{Mini Visual Audit Checklist}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Does the title describe what is plotted without overstating the claim?
\item
  Is it clear whether the statistic is weighted or unweighted?
\item
  Are axes, groups, and units labeled plainly?
\item
  Are missing values or exclusions disclosed?
\item
  Is the scale chosen to support honest comparison rather than
  exaggeration?
\item
  Does the caption state the limitation that this is descriptive, not
  causal?
\end{itemize}
