\chapter*{How a Plot Can Mislead}\label{how-a-plot-can-mislead}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{How a Plot Can Mislead}

\markboth{How a Plot Can Mislead}{How a Plot Can Mislead}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You have a clean, captioned worked example
(\hyperref[worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization]{viz03}). This
page is the audit lens: six common ways a figure that \emph{looks fine}
can quietly mislead a reader. Each one shows up in real research, and
each one is something an AI co-pilot will produce if you do not push
back.

\section*{Six failure modes}\label{six-failure-modes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Six failure modes}

\markright{Six failure modes}

For each failure mode, you will see the problem in one sentence and the
fix in one sentence. Studio time in
\href{viz06-studio-visual-audit.qmd}{viz06} revisits these on a
deliberately flawed plot.

\subsection*{1. Truncated y-axis
exaggeration}\label{truncated-y-axis-exaggeration}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Truncated y-axis exaggeration}

A small difference in means looks huge when the y-axis starts at 27
instead of 0.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} \texttt{coord\_cartesian(ylim\ =\ c(27.5,\ 31.5))}
  on a BMI mean plot, no narrative explanation.
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} Start the axis at 0, or at a value justified in the
  caption. Use \texttt{expand\_limits(y\ =\ 0)} when in doubt.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{2. Causal title on a descriptive
plot}\label{causal-title-on-a-descriptive-plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Causal title on a descriptive plot}

A title that says ``X causes Y'' is a causal claim. Descriptive
summaries cannot support it.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} ``Income level causes BMI to rise across NHANES
  cycles.''
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} ``Mean BMI by income group across NHANES cycles in the
  prepared classroom dataset.''
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Definition: causal overreach}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

A descriptive chart can show a pattern or association, but it does not
prove that one factor caused another. Claiming cause from a descriptive
plot is called causal overreach.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{3. Smoothing across categorical
time}\label{smoothing-across-categorical-time}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Smoothing across categorical time}

\texttt{geom\_smooth(method\ =\ "lm")} draws a line that \emph{implies}
a continuous linear trend. NHANES cycles are categorical labels, not a
continuous axis.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} A linear regression line layered across cycle bins.
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} Use \texttt{geom\_line()} plus \texttt{geom\_point()} to
  connect descriptive cycle summaries, with no regression overlay.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{4. Weighted-vs-unweighted
ambiguity}\label{weighted-vs-unweighted-ambiguity}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Weighted-vs-unweighted ambiguity}

If the subtitle says ``weighted'' but the code does not use a survey
weight variable, the reader has been misled.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} Subtitle: ``Weighted BMI trend.'' Code:
  \texttt{mean(BMI,\ na.rm\ =\ TRUE)} with no weight.
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} State plainly that the summary is unweighted, or compute
  a weighted estimate using the documented weight variable.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{5. Silent missingness}\label{silent-missingness}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. Silent missingness}

Rows with missing BMI or income disappear without a caption.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} \texttt{filter(!is.na(BMI),\ !is.na(IncomeGroup))}
  deep inside a pipe with no note in the caption.
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} Disclose missing-value exclusions in the caption.
  Better: report missingness counts before the figure (you will do this
  on \href{../week07-eda-ai-audit/eda03-worked-example.qmd}{eda03} next
  week).
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{6. Hidden small-cell
counts}\label{hidden-small-cell-counts}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. Hidden small-cell counts}

A group with \texttt{n\ =\ 7} plots the same way as a group with
\texttt{n\ =\ 700}, but the reader cannot tell them apart.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} A small income group plotted as a line with no
  indication of sparse cells.
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} Filter out groups below a minimum count, \emph{or} label
  them visibly with \texttt{n} annotations, \emph{or} report the counts
  beside the figure.
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Definition: hidden small cell count}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

A hidden small cell count is a group with very few observations that may
be hidden inside a chart or summary. Do not make strong claims from
these groups.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Seeing it: a before/after
pair}\label{seeing-it-a-beforeafter-pair}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Seeing it: a before/after pair}

\markright{Seeing it: a before/after pair}

Reading about failure modes is one thing; seeing them side by side is
another. The two figures below use the same summary as the worked
example on
\hyperref[worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization]{viz03}. The
first plot combines failure modes \#1 and \#2. The second plot is the
honest version. (This pair is \emph{not} the studio plot --- the flawed
plot you will audit in \href{viz06-studio-visual-audit.qmd}{viz06} has
its own problems for you to find.)

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tidyverse) }\CommentTok{\# AI{-}EDIT(2026{-}06{-}23): tidyverse{-}default — consolidated core library() calls into library(tidyverse)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{bmi\_by\_income\_cycle }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup), }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(Cycle), Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(Cycle, IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(), }\AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Before --- truncated axis and causal title.} The y-axis starts
near the data, so small gaps look dramatic, and the title makes a claim
the descriptive summary cannot support:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{  bmi\_by\_income\_cycle,}
  \FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Cycle, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ mean\_bmi, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup, }\AttributeTok{group =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup)}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \FloatTok{1.2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{size =} \FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{scale\_color\_brewer}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{palette =} \StringTok{"Dark2"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{coord\_cartesian}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{27.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{31.5}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Income level causes BMI to rise across NHANES cycles"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"NHANES cycle"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Income group"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{base\_size =} \DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{axis.text.x =} \FunctionTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{angle =} \DecValTok{45}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{hjust =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"bottom"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{weeks/week06-visualization/viz04-misleading-design_files/figure-pdf/viz04-before-misleading-1.pdf}}

\textbf{After --- honest scale and descriptive title.} Same data, same
geometry. The axis starts at 0, the title describes instead of claiming
cause, and the caption discloses the exclusions:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{  bmi\_by\_income\_cycle,}
  \FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Cycle, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ mean\_bmi, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup, }\AttributeTok{group =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup)}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \FloatTok{1.2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{size =} \FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{scale\_color\_brewer}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{palette =} \StringTok{"Dark2"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{expand\_limits}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI by income group across NHANES cycles"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"Adults age 20{-}80 in the prepared class dataset; unweighted descriptive summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"NHANES cycle"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Income group"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{caption =} \StringTok{"Records with missing BMI, income, or cycle excluded; groups under n = 30 removed.}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{Descriptive only, not a causal or population estimate."}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{base\_size =} \DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{axis.text.x =} \FunctionTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{angle =} \DecValTok{45}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{hjust =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"bottom"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{weeks/week06-visualization/viz04-misleading-design_files/figure-pdf/viz04-after-corrected-1.pdf}}

Notice what the fix did \emph{not} change: the data, the geometry, and
the colors are identical. Honest visualization is mostly about scale,
title, and disclosure.

\section*{Why six and not sixty}\label{why-six-and-not-sixty}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why six and not sixty}

\markright{Why six and not sixty}

You will see other failure modes in the wild --- duplicated colors,
default rainbow palettes, dual axes, missing legends. The six above
account for most of the issues you will catch when auditing an
AI-drafted plot for descriptive health data work in this course. Master
these first.

\section*{A quick audit habit}\label{a-quick-audit-habit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A quick audit habit}

\markright{A quick audit habit}

When you finish a plot, ask three questions out loud:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Could a reader make a causal claim from this title or caption?
\item
  Could a small difference look bigger than it is because of the axis?
\item
  Is missingness, exclusion, or small-cell count visible to the reader?
\end{enumerate}

If any answer is ``yes'' or ``I don't know,'' revise the figure or the
caption before moving on.

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{viz05-ai-prompts-and-audit.qmd}{viz05} shows how to write AI
prompts that produce plots you can defend, and how to audit what the AI
gives you.

\chapter*{AI Prompts You Can Defend}\label{ai-prompts-you-can-defend}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{AI Prompts You Can Defend}

\markboth{AI Prompts You Can Defend}{AI Prompts You Can Defend}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You can build a ggplot from scratch
(\hyperref[grammar-of-a-ggplot-and-your-first-plot]{viz02}) and you can
spot six common failure modes
(\hyperref[how-a-plot-can-mislead]{viz04}). This page connects the two:
how to ask an AI co-pilot for plot code without inheriting its bad
defaults, and how to audit what it gives you.

\section*{Why this matters}\label{why-this-matters-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why this matters}

\markright{Why this matters}

If you paste ``make me a nice plot of NHANES'' into a chat window, the
model will produce something --- possibly with a flashy palette, a trend
line, and a confident title. None of those choices were yours. None of
them were audited. By the time the figure is in your report, you own
them anyway.

The fix is to write prompts that \textbf{constrain the model} instead of
decorate the request.

\section*{Three prompt templates that
work}\label{three-prompt-templates-that-work}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Three prompt templates that work}

\markright{Three prompt templates that work}

\subsection*{Template 1 --- Start from the data, not the
picture}\label{template-1-start-from-the-data-not-the-picture}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Template 1 --- Start from the data,
not the picture}

\begin{quote}
I have a data frame called \texttt{bmi\_by\_income\_cycle} with columns
\texttt{Cycle}, \texttt{IncomeGroup}, \texttt{n}, and
\texttt{mean\_bmi}. Write a \texttt{ggplot2} figure that shows
\texttt{mean\_bmi} on the y-axis and \texttt{Cycle} on the x-axis, with
one line per \texttt{IncomeGroup}. The summary is unweighted and
descriptive. Do not add a trend line or smoothing.
\end{quote}

What this prompt does well: it names the data, the variables, the
geometry, and what \emph{not} to add.

\subsection*{Template 2 --- Ask for an honest scale and
labels}\label{template-2-ask-for-an-honest-scale-and-labels}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Template 2 --- Ask for an honest scale
and labels}

\begin{quote}
Add \texttt{labs(...)} to the figure above so the title describes the
comparison without making a causal claim, the subtitle states that the
summary is unweighted and uses adults age 20-80, and the caption
discloses that records with missing BMI, income, or cycle were excluded.
\end{quote}

What this prompt does well: it tells the model what each label should
\emph{do}, not just that there should be labels.

\subsection*{Template 3 --- Ask for a caption that names the
limitation}\label{template-3-ask-for-a-caption-that-names-the-limitation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Template 3 --- Ask for a caption that
names the limitation}

\begin{quote}
Write a one-sentence caption for this figure that names the statistic
(mean BMI), the comparison (across NHANES cycles, by income group), the
data source (prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom CSV), the
exclusions (missing values, ages outside 20-80), and one limitation
(descriptive only, not a population estimate).
\end{quote}

What this prompt does well: the model now has a checklist of what the
caption must contain.

\section*{Auditing what the model gives
you}\label{auditing-what-the-model-gives-you}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Auditing what the model gives you}

\markright{Auditing what the model gives you}

After you paste in any AI-drafted plot code, walk through this checklist
before you accept it:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Column names match the data.} Run \texttt{glimpse(your\_data)}
  and compare.
\item
  \textbf{Geometry is appropriate.} No smoothing across categorical
  cycles. No regression lines on a descriptive summary.
\item
  \textbf{Scale is honest.} Does the y-axis start at 0, or is there a
  stated reason it does not?
\item
  \textbf{Title and subtitle are descriptive.} No ``X causes Y''
  wording.
\item
  \textbf{Caption discloses exclusions and limitation.} Missing values,
  cohort restrictions, weighted vs unweighted.
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{na.rm\ =\ TRUE} is present} wherever the summary uses
  a numeric column that may have \texttt{NA}.
\end{enumerate}

If any item fails, edit the code or the labels before rendering.

\section*{The paper-trail rule (still in
force)}\label{the-paper-trail-rule-still-in-force}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The paper-trail rule (still in force)}

\markright{The paper-trail rule (still in force)}

From Week 3: every AI-drafted chunk in your \texttt{.qmd} gets a
two-line comment.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\CommentTok{\# AI prompt: "Plot mean\_bmi by Cycle, one line per IncomeGroup, no smoothing"}
\CommentTok{\# Verified: columns match glimpse(); no smoother; descriptive title; caption lists exclusions}
\NormalTok{bmi\_plot }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(bmi\_by\_income\_cycle, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(...)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ ...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This is the same audit log you used in Week 3 for data wrangling. The
course AI policy requires it for graded work.

\section*{A note on what NOT to ask the
AI}\label{a-note-on-what-not-to-ask-the-ai}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A note on what NOT to ask the AI}

\markright{A note on what NOT to ask the AI}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Do not paste row-level NHANES data into an external chat window.
  Aggregate first.
\item
  Do not ask the AI to ``decide'' whether income causes BMI. The figure
  is descriptive; causal language is your responsibility to remove.
\item
  Do not accept a ``looks fine to me'' from the model as audit evidence.
  The audit is yours.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{viz06-studio-visual-audit.qmd}{viz06} is the in-class studio where
you apply all of this to a deliberately flawed plot.

\chapter*{In-Class Studio: AI Visual
Audit}\label{in-class-studio-ai-visual-audit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{In-Class Studio: AI Visual Audit}

\markboth{In-Class Studio: AI Visual Audit}{In-Class Studio: AI Visual
Audit}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You have the worked example
(\hyperref[worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization]{viz03}), the
audit categories (\hyperref[how-a-plot-can-mislead]{viz04}), and the
AI-prompt pattern (\hyperref[ai-prompts-you-can-defend]{viz05}). This
page is the in-class studio where you put all three to work: read a
deliberately flawed plot, identify at least three problems, revise the
plot, and write a short audit note. The same workflow is what Assignment
5 expects, so think of this as the dress rehearsal.

\section*{Before you start}\label{before-you-start}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Before you start}

\markright{Before you start}

Take 2 minutes to check that your Codespace is ready:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{library(tidyverse)} runs without error.
\item
  The cached data file resolves from the repository root:
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}.
\item
  The rendered
  \hyperref[worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization]{Week 6 worked
  example} opens in the course book; you do not need its source file in
  your personal workspace.
\end{enumerate}

If any of those three fail, raise it with your debugging partner before
moving on.

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Evaluate one AI-generated visualization for correctness, clarity, bias,
aggregation choices, and misleading design. Then revise it so the visual
matches the evidence.

\section*{Inputs}\label{inputs}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Inputs}

\markright{Inputs}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Worked example to compare against:
  \hyperref[worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization]{\texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/viz03-worked-example.qmd}}
\item
  Dataset:
  \href{https://github.com/ehsanx/HDSx/blob/main/examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes_equity_v6.csv}{\texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
  (view on GitHub)}
\item
  Flawed plot code in your personal workspace (the artifact you will
  audit):
  \texttt{assignments/assignment05-visualization/flawed-plot-code.R}
\end{itemize}

\section*{Activity Steps}\label{activity-steps}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Activity Steps}

\markright{Activity Steps}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Review the worked example and note how it labels the data source,
  statistic, and limitation.
\item
  In your personal workspace, open
  \texttt{assignments/assignment05-visualization/flawed-plot-code.R} and
  read the comments and output labels before you run it.
\item
  Run the script from your personal workspace root with
  \texttt{source("assignments/assignment05-visualization/flawed-plot-code.R")}.
\item
  Look at the figure. It may appear in a plot viewer pane. If it does
  not, no problem: the script also saves \texttt{flawed-plot.png} in
  your current working folder (run \texttt{getwd()} if you are unsure
  where that is). Find the PNG in the Explorer, right-click it, and
  choose \textbf{Open Preview}.
\item
  List at least three issues that could mislead a reader. Tag each one
  with one of the audit categories below.
\item
  Revise the plot so the title, scale, grouping, and caption match the
  evidence. Keep the cohort restriction explicit.
\item
  Write a short note explaining what you changed and why.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Audit Categories}\label{audit-categories}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Audit Categories}

\markright{Audit Categories}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Correctness:} Does the plot show what the title and labels
  claim?
\item
  \textbf{Design:} Do scale, color, ordering, and geometry support fair
  comparison?
\item
  \textbf{Interpretation:} Does the caption avoid causal or
  population-level claims that the plot cannot support?
\item
  \textbf{Bias and aggregation:} Could grouping, missingness, or small
  counts hide important variation?
\item
  \textbf{Reproducibility:} Can another person rerun the code from the
  repository root using relative paths?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Required Outputs (studio
draft)}\label{required-outputs-studio-draft}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Required Outputs (studio draft)}

\markright{Required Outputs (studio draft)}

These are the draft files you produce in class. The Assignment 5
deliverable list (\href{viz07-assignment-a5-and-reference.qmd}{viz07})
is the same set, polished.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{visual-audit-note.md} --- at least three issues with category,
  why it matters, and the corrected approach.
\item
  \texttt{visualization-audit.qmd} --- a rerunnable Quarto file that
  creates the corrected plot and explains the revision.
\item
  \texttt{corrected-plot.png} --- the exported corrected plot.
\item
  \texttt{caption.md} --- a caption that states the statistic, data
  source, exclusions, and limitations.
\item
  \texttt{ai-use-note.md} --- if you used AI, explain what it helped
  with and how you checked the result.
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{What a strong audit-note entry looks like}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

Each entry in \texttt{visual-audit-note.md} should give this level of
evidence. This example is generic --- it is \textbf{not} one of the
planted issues in this studio's plot:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Issue:} the draft layered
  \texttt{geom\_smooth(method\ =\ "lm")} across NHANES cycles.
\item
  \textbf{Category:} design.
\item
  \textbf{Why it matters:} cycles are categorical labels, so a
  regression line implies a continuous trend the data cannot support.
\item
  \textbf{Correction:} replaced the smoother with
  \texttt{geom\_line()\ +\ geom\_point()} connecting the cycle
  summaries.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Completion Check}\label{completion-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Completion Check}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

You have the worked example, the failure modes, the prompt templates,
and the studio drafts. This page is the bridge from in-class work to the

in the \href{../../assignments/assignment05-visualization/README.md}{A5

\section*{Deadline and link to M1}\label{deadline-and-link-to-m1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Deadline and link to M1}

\markright{Deadline and link to M1}

Assignment 5 is due the Monday after Week 6 class, 4 PM. The Milestone 1
(Project Proposal) \texttt{initial-visual-plan.md} is due the same
Monday. The two pieces of work share a standard of transparency, but
they are \emph{different} artifacts: A5 polishes a plot from the NHANES
classroom CSV; M1 sketches one planned figure for your team's project.
Do not conflate them.

\subsection*{How the audit habit carries into your M1
proposal}\label{how-the-audit-habit-carries-into-your-m1-proposal}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{How the audit habit carries into your
M1 proposal}

The five audit categories you practiced in
\hyperref[in-class-studio-ai-visual-audit]{viz06} map directly onto the
evidence and claims in your M1 proposal:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Correctness:} the claims in your proposal must match what your
  chosen dataset can actually show.
\item
  \textbf{Design:} the planned figure in \texttt{initial-visual-plan.md}
  should name the statistic and the comparison, not just promise ``a
  chart.''
\item
  \textbf{Interpretation:} write proposal claims as descriptive (``we
  will describe\ldots{}'') rather than causal (``we will show X causes
  Y'').
\item
  \textbf{Bias and aggregation:} say which groups in your dataset may be
  small or missing, and how your team will disclose that.
\item
  \textbf{Reproducibility:} commit to relative paths and rerunnable code
  from the first proposal draft.
\end{itemize}

Your \texttt{m1-link-note.md} (deliverable 6 below) is where you record
this connection in your own words.

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{1.
\texttt{visualization-audit.qmd}}{1. visualization-audit.qmd}}\label{visualization-audit.qmd}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. \texttt{visualization-audit.qmd}}

A rerunnable Quarto file that loads the cached CSV with a relative path,
prepares a descriptive summary, renders the final corrected plot, and
includes one or two short paragraphs explaining what you revised and
why.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} open it in a fresh Codespace, render top to bottom,
  no errors.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{2.
\texttt{visualization-audit.html}}{2. visualization-audit.html}}\label{visualization-audit.html}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. \texttt{visualization-audit.html}}

The rendered output of the \texttt{.qmd} above. Quarto writes this when
you click Render.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} open the HTML in the Codespaces preview and confirm
  the figure, caption, and audit paragraph all appear.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{3.
\texttt{corrected-plot.png}}{3. corrected-plot.png}}\label{corrected-plot.png}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. \texttt{corrected-plot.png}}

The exported corrected plot as a static image, written from code rather
than by screenshot.

To save your corrected ggplot, use:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{ggsave}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"assignments/assignment05{-}visualization/submission/corrected{-}plot.png"}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{plot =} \FunctionTok{last\_plot}\NormalTok{(),}
  \AttributeTok{width =} \DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{height =} \DecValTok{5}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Confirm the file appears in the submission folder before committing.

Step by step:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Run the code that draws your corrected plot, so it is the most recent
  figure in your session.
\item
  Run the \texttt{ggsave()} call above. \texttt{plot\ =\ last\_plot()}
  means ``the most recent ggplot I drew.'' If you stored the plot in a
  variable, pass that instead: \texttt{plot\ =\ my\_corrected\_plot}.
\item
  The path starts from the repository root, so run it from there. If
  your R session is already inside the submission folder, shorten the
  path to \texttt{"corrected-plot.png"}.
\item
  Check the result in VS Code: find the PNG in the Explorer panel,
  right-click it, and choose \textbf{Open Preview} to confirm it opens
  and looks right.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} the PNG exists, opens, and matches what the rendered
  HTML shows.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{4.
\texttt{visual-audit-note.md}}{4. visual-audit-note.md}}\label{visual-audit-note.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. \texttt{visual-audit-note.md}}

A short note listing at least three issues you identified in the flawed
plot from \hyperref[in-class-studio-ai-visual-audit]{viz06}. For each
issue, write one sentence on what it was, one sentence on why it
matters, and one sentence on how you corrected it.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} three issues, each tagged with a category from
  \hyperref[how-a-plot-can-mislead]{viz04} (correctness, design,
  interpretation, bias/aggregation, or reproducibility).
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{5.
\texttt{caption.md}}{5. caption.md}}\label{caption.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. \texttt{caption.md}}

The caption for your final figure. Use the template from
\hyperref[worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization]{viz03}:

\begin{quote}
This figure shows {[}measure{]} by {[}group{]} across
{[}time/category{]} using the prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom
dataset. The summary is descriptive and unweighted. Records with missing
{[}variables{]} were excluded. The plot supports comparison of patterns,
not causal claims or population-level estimates.
\end{quote}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} the caption names the statistic, the data source, the
  exclusions, and one limitation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{6. \texttt{m1-link-note.md} {[}brief ---
1-3 focused bullets or sentences are enough{]}
}{6. m1-link-note.md {[}brief --- 1-3 focused bullets or sentences are enough{]} }}\label{m1-link-note.md-brief-1-3-focused-bullets-or-sentences-are-enough}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. \texttt{m1-link-note.md} {[}brief
--- 1-3 focused bullets or sentences are enough{]} }

Two or three sentences connecting the visualization standard from this
assignment to your M1 visual plan. What did this assignment change about
the figure your team plans to make? Did it change your dataset choice,
the exclusions you will document, or the caption your team will write?

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} the note is specific to \emph{your team's} project,
  not generic advice.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{7.
\texttt{ai-use-note.md}}{7. ai-use-note.md}}\label{ai-use-note.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{7. \texttt{ai-use-note.md}}

If you used AI to draft code or wording, name what it helped with and
how you verified the result. If you did not use AI, write one sentence
saying so.

Copy your inline \texttt{\#\ AI\ prompt:} / \texttt{\#\ Verified:}
comment pairs into the note's audit trail. The template and a filled
example are on the
\hyperref[the-official-ai-documentation-rule]{assignments index}.

A5}\label{reproducibility-checklist-for-a5}

\section*{Accessibility (good practice, not a graded
deliverable)}\label{accessibility-good-practice-not-a-graded-deliverable}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Accessibility (good practice, not a
graded deliverable)}

\markright{Accessibility (good practice, not a graded deliverable)}

A plain-language alt text sentence next to your figure helps
screen-reader users, colour-blind reviewers, and busy policymakers. The
A5 rubric does not score alt text --- it is encouraged practice. If you
want to try it, add \texttt{fig-alt:} as a chunk option:

\section*{ggplot2 quick reference}\label{ggplot2-quick-reference}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{ggplot2 quick reference}

\markright{ggplot2 quick reference}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Need
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Code
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Note
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Load packages & \texttt{library(tidyverse)} & Every session \\
Set data + mapping & \texttt{ggplot(df,\ aes(x,\ y))} & The starting
point \\
Points & \texttt{geom\_point()} & Use \texttt{alpha} for overlap \\
Lines connecting summaries & \texttt{geom\_line()\ +\ geom\_point()} &
Better than \texttt{geom\_smooth()} for descriptive cycle data \\
Bars / columns & \texttt{geom\_col()} & For pre-aggregated counts \\
Labels & \texttt{labs(title,\ subtitle,\ x,\ y,\ caption)} & Caption
goes under the figure \\
Honest y-axis & \texttt{expand\_limits(y\ =\ 0)} & Start at zero unless
you justify otherwise \\
Theme & \texttt{theme\_minimal()} & Clean default \\
Save figure &
\texttt{ggsave("plot.png",\ plot,\ width\ =\ 6,\ height\ =\ 4)} &
Code-generated, not screenshot; for A5 use the full submission-folder
path (see deliverable 3) \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Visual audit checklist}\label{visual-audit-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Visual audit checklist}

\markright{Visual audit checklist}

A short version of the five questions from
\hyperref[how-a-plot-can-mislead]{viz04}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Title and caption do not imply causation.
\item
  Y-axis starts at 0 or has a stated reason.
\item
  Geometry suits descriptive data (no smoother across categorical x).
\item
  Weighted vs unweighted is stated.
\item
  Missingness and exclusions are disclosed.
\item
  Small-cell counts are visible or filtered out.
\end{itemize}

\part{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \& AI Auditing}

\chapter*{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \& AI
Auditing}\label{week-7-eda-table-1-ai-auditing-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \& AI Auditing}

\markboth{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \& AI Auditing}{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \&
AI Auditing}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

This week builds the audit trail behind a descriptive analysis. You will
define an analysis cohort, check missingness, create a Table 1-style
summary, and audit a planted-error starter that mimics common
AI-assisted mistakes.

A Table 1 is a descriptive summary of the cohort before the main
analysis. It usually shows who is included, key variables, missingness,
and basic group summaries. It is not a causal result. Worked examples of
every piece --- cohort definition, exclusions, the missingness table,
complete-case analysis, and a sample Table 1 --- are in
\href{eda02-cohort-and-missingness.qmd}{eda02} and
\href{eda03-worked-example.qmd}{eda03}.

EDA is treated as \textbf{evidence stewardship}: every exclusion,
statistic, and interpretation should be visible enough for another
person to rerun and challenge.

This week also revisits data provenance and Indigenous Data Sovereignty.
The stewardship audit category
(\href{eda05-ai-audit-categories.qmd}{eda05}) asks the same questions
you met with OCAP and CARE in
\hyperref[indigenous-data-sovereignty]{Week 1}: who has rights over this
data, and does our use respect them?

\begin{itemize}

  6} files, explained one by one in

  \href{../../milestones/m2-preliminary-analysis/README.md}{M2
  preliminary analysis} due the same Monday.
\item
  \textbf{Draft (studio work that feeds A6, not graded on its own):} the
  EDA note, missingness table, Table 1, and planted-error audit from
  \href{eda06-studio-planted-errors.qmd}{eda06}.
\item
  \textbf{Optional:} nothing extra this week --- every listed file is
  required.
\end{itemize}

Tags like \texttt{{[}required{]}} and \texttt{{[}optional{]}} are
defined in \hyperref[how-to-read-submission-lists]{How To Read
Submission Lists}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Objectives}\label{objectives-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Objectives}

\markright{Objectives}

By the end of the week you can:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  define and document an analysis cohort before summarizing;
\item
  report missingness for key variables rather than dropping rows
  silently;
\item
  create a Table 1-style descriptive summary with N, mean, and SD;
\item
  distinguish what descriptive statistics claim from what they cannot
  claim;
\item
  audit code and prose for correctness, reproducibility, interpretation,
  and stewardship errors.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 6 focused on whether visual design makes a descriptive claim honest
and clear. Week 7 documents the cohort, missingness, and summary
statistics behind those claims. Week 8 then turns one audited
descriptive finding into a dashboard-style KT prototype.

\section*{Case Study Data Analysis}\label{case-study-data-analysis-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Case Study Data Analysis}

\markright{Case Study Data Analysis}

The \textbf{NHANES Health Equity data spine} now supports code-reading,
EDA, visualization, and reproducibility checks. Use the cached CSV/RDS
for routine class work; a CDC retrieval script exists for advanced
users.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Cached RDS:
  \href{https://github.com/ehsanx/HDSx/blob/main/examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes_equity_v6.rds}{\texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.rds}}
\item
  CSV snapshot:
  \href{https://github.com/ehsanx/HDSx/blob/main/examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes_equity_v6.csv}{\texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}}
\item
  Case-study README:
  \href{https://github.com/ehsanx/HDSx/blob/main/examples/nhanes-equity/README.md}{\texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/README.md}}
\end{itemize}

The default classroom path is to use the cached data so the analysis
work is reproducible without internet access.

\textbf{What to do here:} you may run the code below --- it loads the
cached CSV and prints two small summaries; reading it carefully is
enough unless your week's page asks for more.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tidyverse) }\CommentTok{\# AI{-}EDIT(2026{-}06{-}23): tidyverse{-}default — consolidated core library() calls into library(tidyverse)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(nhanes\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(nhanes\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{rows =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_income =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup))}
\NormalTok{  )}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 1 x 3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}     rows missing\_bmi missing\_income}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    \textless{}int\textgreater{}       \textless{}int\textgreater{}          \textless{}int\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 1 105626        9356           9415}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup), Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 3 x 3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}   IncomeGroup            n mean\_bmi}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}   \textless{}chr\textgreater{}              \textless{}int\textgreater{}    \textless{}dbl\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 1 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5) 16330     28.6}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 2 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)  15293     29.4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 3 Middle Income      19228     29.3}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Definition of done}\label{definition-of-done-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definition of done}

\markright{Definition of done}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Cohort restrictions are stated before results are interpreted.
\item
  Missingness is checked and reported before complete-case summaries are
  used.
\item
  Table labels match the statistic actually computed.
\item
  The audit identifies at least five planted issues across correctness,
  reproducibility, interpretation, and stewardship.
\item
  M2 preliminary analysis renders from source and explains what the
  descriptive results do not prove.
\end{itemize}

\section*{What students leave with}\label{what-students-leave-with-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What students leave with}

\markright{What students leave with}

A reproducible EDA note and a habit of treating AI-generated summaries
as drafts that must be checked against the data, code, and stewardship
context.

\chapter*{EDA as Evidence
Stewardship}\label{eda-as-evidence-stewardship}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{EDA as Evidence Stewardship}

\markboth{EDA as Evidence Stewardship}{EDA as Evidence Stewardship}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Last week you turned a descriptive summary into a clear figure. This
week you build the evidence trail behind that summary. The phrase
``exploratory data analysis'' often sounds casual --- \emph{``just look
at the data''}. In a health-data project it is not casual at all: every
exclusion, every statistic, and every interpretation should be visible
enough that another person can rerun your work and challenge it.

Think of EDA as the \textbf{paperwork} that defends a finding. If the
paperwork is missing, the finding cannot survive an audit.

\section*{The four artifacts this week
produces}\label{the-four-artifacts-this-week-produces}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The four artifacts this week produces}

\markright{The four artifacts this week produces}

Every page this week supports one of these four:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A \textbf{cohort definition} that names exactly who is in the analysis
  sample.
\item
  A \textbf{missingness table} that says how much of each key variable
  is missing.
\item
  A \textbf{Table 1} that summarizes the cohort with N, mean (SD), and
  equivalents.
\item
  A \textbf{methods note} that states what the table can and cannot
  claim.
\end{enumerate}

If any one of the four is absent, the work is not ready for Milestone 2
or Assignment 6.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Terms This Week}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Cohort:} the group of records included in the analysis after
  stated restrictions --- the one-sentence answer to ``who is in this
  analysis?''
\item
  \textbf{Missingness:} how many values are absent for each variable you
  use. Report it before summarizing, never after.
\item
  \textbf{Complete-case analysis:} keeping only rows with no missing
  values for the variables used. Acceptable only when the missingness
  counts are reported first.
\item
  \textbf{Table 1:} the standard descriptive summary table of a cohort
  --- N, mean (SD), and similar statistics by group --- produced before
  any main analysis.
\end{itemize}

Each term is unpacked on \href{eda02-cohort-and-missingness.qmd}{eda02}
and in the \href{eda03-worked-example.qmd}{worked example}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Why this matters for AI
work}\label{why-this-matters-for-ai-work}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why this matters for AI work}

\markright{Why this matters for AI work}

When you ask an AI co-pilot for ``a Table 1,'' the model will produce
one. It will not check whether the cohort makes sense, whether
missingness was reported first, or whether the labels match the
statistics. Those are your responsibilities. The four-artifact workflow
is what makes that responsibility manageable: each artifact is a small
piece you can audit on its own.

The four audit categories you will use this week ---
\textbf{correctness, reproducibility, interpretation, stewardship} ---
are introduced on \href{eda05-ai-audit-categories.qmd}{eda05} and
practiced in the \href{eda06-studio-planted-errors.qmd}{studio}.

\section*{What ``descriptive, unweighted, non-causal''
means}\label{what-descriptive-unweighted-non-causal-means}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What ``descriptive, unweighted,
non-causal'' means}

\markright{What ``descriptive, unweighted, non-causal'' means}

You will see this phrase repeatedly. Each word matters:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Descriptive:} the table reports what is in the data, not what
  the population looks like.
\item
  \textbf{Unweighted:} we are not using NHANES survey weights, so the
  numbers describe the prepared classroom dataset, not US adults.
\item
  \textbf{Non-causal:} the table cannot tell you that one variable
  \emph{causes} another. Descriptive comparison is not causal inference.
\end{itemize}

Saying this in your methods note is how you defend the table from being
misread.

\section*{Where AI fits}\label{where-ai-fits-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where AI fits}

\markright{Where AI fits}

Same rule as Week 3 and Week 6: every AI-drafted chunk in your
\texttt{.qmd} gets a two-line
\texttt{\#\ AI\ prompt:\ ...\ \#\ Verified:\ ...} comment. The
verification step on Table 1 work specifically checks: cohort filter,
missingness handling, label/statistic match, and interpretation.

\section*{What you produce this
week}\label{what-you-produce-this-week-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What you produce this week}

\markright{What you produce this week}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A rerunnable \texttt{eda-note.qmd} with cohort definition, missingness
  table, and Table 1 (the Assignment 6 deliverable, also the M2
  preliminary analysis).
\item
  A short audit note identifying at least five issues in a planted-error
  starter.
\item
  A revised provenance/stewardship statement suitable for reuse in M2.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{eda02-cohort-and-missingness.qmd}{eda02} walks through cohort
definition and missingness reporting --- the first two of the four
artifacts.

\chapter*{Cohort and Missingness}\label{cohort-and-missingness}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Cohort and Missingness}

\markboth{Cohort and Missingness}{Cohort and Missingness}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

\hyperref[eda-as-evidence-stewardship]{eda01} named the four artifacts
your EDA work produces. This page covers the first two: how to define
and document an analysis cohort, and how to report missingness
\emph{before} you summarize anything. Both habits are the foundation of
every Table 1 you will make for the rest of the term.

\section*{Why cohort definition comes
first}\label{why-cohort-definition-comes-first}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why cohort definition comes first}

\markright{Why cohort definition comes first}

A dataset is not an analysis sample. The NHANES Health Equity CSV
contains records of all ages, with some variables intentionally coded as
\texttt{NA}. If you summarize without restricting, you are answering a
question the data cannot answer cleanly.

A cohort definition is a one-sentence answer to \emph{``who is in this
analysis?''} --- written down before you compute any statistics. Common
examples:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  ``Adults age 20-80 in the prepared classroom dataset.''
\item
  ``Female respondents with non-missing BMI.''
\item
  ``Records from NHANES cycles 2013-2014 and 2015-2016.''
\end{itemize}

Each restriction has a reason. The reason goes in the methods note.

\section*{Documenting the cohort in
code}\label{documenting-the-cohort-in-code}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Documenting the cohort in code}

\markright{Documenting the cohort in code}

This pattern shows up in the \href{eda03-worked-example.qmd}{worked
example}. You will reuse it for A6 and M2.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\CommentTok{\# AI{-}EDIT(2026{-}06{-}23): tidyverse{-}default — consolidated core library() calls into library(tidyverse)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tidyverse)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(knitr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{analysis\_df }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{cohort\_counts }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{step =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Prepared CSV rows"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"After age 20{-}80 restriction"}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(nhanes), }\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df))}
\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(cohort\_counts)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{cohort\_counts} table is your \textbf{flow diagram in text
form}. It shows N before and after each restriction. If a peer asks
``how many people are in your analysis?'', this table is the answer.

\section*{Why missingness comes before the
summary}\label{why-missingness-comes-before-the-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why missingness comes before the summary}

\markright{Why missingness comes before the summary}

A complete-case summary silently drops rows. If you compute
\texttt{mean(BMI,\ na.rm\ =\ TRUE)} without first showing how many BMI
values were missing, your reader has no idea how representative the mean
is.

The audit habit is: \textbf{report missingness first, then summarize,
then interpret.} Never the other order.

\section*{Counting missingness}\label{counting-missingness}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Counting missingness}

\markright{Counting missingness}

The cleanest beginner pattern:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{key\_vars }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"BMI"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Age"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"IncomeGroup"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Gender"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Race"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Cycle"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{missingness }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{variable =}\NormalTok{ key\_vars) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_n =} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df[key\_vars], }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(x)), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_percent =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ missing\_n }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(missingness)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This produces a small table: one row per key variable, with the missing
count and the percent missing. Three rules apply when you write or
accept code like this:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Pick \texttt{key\_vars} deliberately.} Only include variables
  you will actually use in the Table 1 or downstream summary.
\item
  \textbf{Use the denominator from the cohort, not the raw dataset.}
  Missingness as a percent of \emph{your cohort} tells the right story.
\item
  \textbf{Report it, even if it is small.} A row that says ``BMI
  missing: 2 (0.1\%)'' is still informative.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{What ``complete-case'' means and why it can bias the
summary}\label{what-complete-case-means-and-why-it-can-bias-the-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What ``complete-case'' means and why it
can bias the summary}

\markright{What ``complete-case'' means and why it can bias the summary}

After you see the missingness table, you have a choice. The simplest
path is \textbf{complete-case analysis}: drop rows where any key
variable is missing. This is what most beginner tutorials show.

That choice is reasonable for a classroom Table 1, but it is not
automatically unbiased. If the rows you drop are systematically
different from the rows you keep (for example, people with missing
income may be different from people with reported income), your summary
tilts in a direction you cannot see from the table alone.

The honest move is to:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Report missingness \emph{first}.
\item
  Filter to complete cases \emph{with the missingness disclosed}.
\item
  Add a sentence in the methods note acknowledging the complete-case
  assumption.
\end{enumerate}

You will see this exact sequence in the
\href{eda03-worked-example.qmd}{worked example}.

\section*{A short audit habit}\label{a-short-audit-habit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A short audit habit}

\markright{A short audit habit}

After you produce a cohort plus missingness table, ask three questions:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Could a reader tell who was excluded and why?
\item
  Is the percent missing reported for every variable in the downstream
  Table 1?
\item
  Did I describe the cohort \emph{before} I described the summary?
\end{enumerate}

If any answer is ``no'', revise before the table.

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{eda03-worked-example.qmd}{eda03} is the first end-to-end worked
example: cohort, missingness, Table 1, methods note --- all four
artifacts in one rerunnable Quarto document.

\chapter*{Worked Example: EDA and Table
1}\label{worked-example-eda-and-table-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Worked Example: EDA and Table 1}

\markboth{Worked Example: EDA and Table 1}{Worked Example: EDA and Table
1}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You met the four-artifact workflow on
\hyperref[eda-as-evidence-stewardship]{eda01} and practiced cohort
definition and missingness reporting on
\hyperref[cohort-and-missingness]{eda02}. This page is the first
end-to-end EDA of the term: load the cached CSV, restrict to an adult
cohort, report missingness, produce a Table 1, and write a short methods
note that survives audit. The same workflow underwrites Assignment 6 and
Milestone 2.

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

This worked example creates a small Table 1-style descriptive summary
from the NHANES Health Equity CSV snapshot. The goal is transparent EDA:
define the cohort, check missingness, summarize key variables, and write
a methods note that does not overclaim.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tidyverse)  }\CommentTok{\# AI{-}EDIT(2026{-}06{-}23): tidyverse{-}default — consolidated core library() calls into library(tidyverse)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(knitr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(data\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Define the Analysis Cohort}\label{define-the-analysis-cohort}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Define the Analysis Cohort}

\markright{Define the Analysis Cohort}

Decision: restrict to adults age 20-80. This keeps the example focused
on adult BMI summaries and avoids mixing children, adolescents, and the
oldest age-coded records into one classroom table.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{analysis\_df }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{cohort\_counts }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{step =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Prepared CSV rows"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Rows after age 20{-}80 restriction"}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(nhanes), }\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df))}
\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(cohort\_counts)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
step & n \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Prepared CSV rows & 105626 \\
Rows after age 20-80 restriction & 57137 \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Missingness Check}\label{missingness-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Missingness Check}

\markright{Missingness Check}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{key\_vars }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"BMI"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Age"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"IncomeGroup"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Gender"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Race"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Cycle"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{missingness }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{variable =}\NormalTok{ key\_vars) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_n =} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df[key\_vars], }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(x)), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_percent =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ missing\_n }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(missingness)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
variable & missing\_n & missing\_percent \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
BMI & 1009 & 1.8 \\
Age & 0 & 0.0 \\
IncomeGroup & 5395 & 9.4 \\
Gender & 0 & 0.0 \\
Race & 0 & 0.0 \\
Cycle & 0 & 0.0 \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Table 1-Style Summary}\label{table-1-style-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Table 1-Style Summary}

\markright{Table 1-Style Summary}

This example stratifies by \texttt{IncomeGroup} and reports N plus mean
(SD) for BMI and age. It is unweighted and descriptive.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{mean\_sd }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) \{}
  \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"\%.1f (\%.1f)"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(x, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{), }\FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(x, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{table1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ analysis\_df }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{N =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{BMI, mean (SD)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean\_sd}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Age, mean (SD)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean\_sd}\NormalTok{(Age),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(table1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
IncomeGroup & N & BMI, mean (SD) & Age, mean (SD) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
High Income (\textgreater3.5) & 16520 & 28.6 (6.3) & 49.7 (16.0) \\
Low Income (\textless1.3) & 15646 & 29.4 (7.5) & 47.4 (18.1) \\
Middle Income & 19576 & 29.3 (7.0) & 50.0 (18.4) \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Methods Note Template}\label{methods-note-template}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Methods Note Template}

\markright{Methods Note Template}

\begin{quote}
We used the prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom CSV snapshot. The
analysis cohort included records with age 20-80. We summarized BMI and
age by income group using unweighted descriptive statistics. Missing
values were checked before summarizing. These summaries describe the
prepared class dataset and should not be interpreted as causal effects
or population estimates.
\end{quote}

\section*{Stewardship Note Template}\label{stewardship-note-template}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Stewardship Note Template}

\markright{Stewardship Note Template}

\begin{quote}
The dataset is a prepared classroom snapshot derived from public-use
NHANES files. It should be used for descriptive learning activities, not
for individual-level inference or small-cell claims. Reports should cite
the source, describe exclusions, and avoid uploading row-level records
to external AI tools.
\end{quote}

\section*{Survey Design Note}\label{survey-design-note}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Survey Design Note}

\markright{Survey Design Note}

NHANES has a complex survey design. This classroom Table 1 is
intentionally unweighted so that the workflow is easy to audit. A
population-representative NHANES analysis would require the appropriate
weights, strata, and primary sampling units.

\section*{Reproducibility Notes}\label{reproducibility-notes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Reproducibility Notes}

\markright{Reproducibility Notes}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The data path is relative to the repository.
\item
  The cohort restriction is stated before the table.
\item
  Missingness is reported before complete-case summaries.
\item
  The table labels distinguish N, mean, and SD.
\item
  The methods note states that the analysis is descriptive and
  non-causal.
\end{itemize}

\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

to ask what each statistic in this table actually \emph{claims}.
