\markright{The nine A7 deliverables, explained}

Each file goes in
\texttt{assignments/assignment07-dashboard/submission/}.

\texttt{testing-log.md}, \texttt{limitation-note.md}, and
\texttt{privacy-check.md} are brief evidence files. One to three focused
sentences or bullets are enough unless the assignment asks for more.

All nine files are {[}required{]}. The
\href{../../assignments/assignment07-dashboard/README.md}{A7 README}
marks the brief ones with {[}brief --- 1-3 focused bullets or sentences
are enough{]}; the full tag vocabulary is in
\hyperref[how-to-read-submission-lists]{How To Read Submission Lists}.

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{1.
\texttt{dashboard.qmd}}{1. dashboard.qmd}}\label{dashboard.qmd}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. \texttt{dashboard.qmd}}

The adapted copy of the personal-workspace Quarto starter. Make the
allowed audience-facing text change here and retain the source with the
evidence files.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} render this exact submitted file in a fresh
  Codespace.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{2.
\texttt{dashboard-adaptation-note.md}}{2. dashboard-adaptation-note.md}}\label{dashboard-adaptation-note.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2.
\texttt{dashboard-adaptation-note.md}}

Audience, question, control, visualization, the element you changed, and
the KT rationale. Pattern after the studio Step 1 and Step 2 outputs.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} a peer can read this and identify your audience and
  question without seeing your code.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{3.
\texttt{before-after-text.md}}{3. before-after-text.md}}\label{before-after-text.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. \texttt{before-after-text.md}}

The original text, the revised text, why the change helps the audience,
and a confirmation that no analytic logic changed. Pattern after the
studio Step 3 output.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} the change is genuinely audience-facing text. If you
  cannot say ``no analytic logic changed'', the change does not belong
  in A7 (see
  \hyperref[audience-facing-text-what-you-can-and-cannot-change-for-a7]{dash04}).
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{4.
\texttt{testing-log.md}}{4. testing-log.md}}\label{testing-log.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. \texttt{testing-log.md}}

Date tested, the command or render path you used, the values you tried,
and the result. Pattern after the studio Step 4 output.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} another student could reproduce your test by
  following your log.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{5.
\texttt{limitation-note.md}}{5. limitation-note.md}}\label{limitation-note.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. \texttt{limitation-note.md}}

One sentence on what the dashboard does not support. Use the
descriptive-vs-causal patterns from
\hyperref[four-audit-categories-for-ai-drafted-eda]{eda05}.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} the sentence belongs \emph{in or beside} the
  dashboard, not just in a separate file.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{6.
\texttt{privacy-check.md}}{6. privacy-check.md}}\label{privacy-check.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. \texttt{privacy-check.md}}

Three sentences from
\hyperref[testing-privacy-safe-publishing-and-the-shiny-demo]{dash05}:
data source and classroom-use boundary, aggregate-only confirmation, no
row-level data shared with AI.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} three sentences, each specific.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{7.
\texttt{dashboard-preview.png}}{7. dashboard-preview.png}}\label{dashboard-preview.png}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{7. \texttt{dashboard-preview.png}}

A screenshot or exported preview of the affected dashboard area (after
the text change).

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} the preview clearly shows the revised audience-facing
  element. No row-level table previews.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{8.
\texttt{reproducibility-note.md}}{8. reproducibility-note.md}}\label{reproducibility-note.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{8. \texttt{reproducibility-note.md}}

One paragraph naming the render command, the relative path, and whether
the dashboard renders in a fresh Codespace.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} the path is relative, and the note matches what
  \texttt{testing-log.md} says.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{9.
\texttt{ai-use-note.md}}{9. ai-use-note.md}}\label{ai-use-note.md-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{9. \texttt{ai-use-note.md}}

What AI helped with (or a sentence saying you did not use it) and how
you verified the result.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} specific (which prompt? which file?), not vague.
\end{itemize}

A7}\label{reproducibility-checklist-for-a7}

\section*{A pathway decision template you can
reuse}\label{a-pathway-decision-template-you-can-reuse}

reuse}

\markright{A pathway decision template you can reuse}

When the term-project conversation comes up later in the term, your team
will face the same pathway choice for the final portfolio. Use this
short template to document the decision:

\begin{quote}
Our audience is \_\_\_\_\_\_. We need \_\_\_\_\_\_ kind of
interactivity. The simplest pathway that serves this need is
\textbf{{[}Quarto dashboard-style page / Shiny app / static HTML/JS{]}}.
{[}If Shiny or static HTML/JS:{]} We have instructor approval recorded
on \_\_\_\_\_\_.
\end{quote}

Default to Quarto. Justify deviations.

\section*{Privacy-safe publishing
checklist}\label{privacy-safe-publishing-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Privacy-safe publishing checklist}

\markright{Privacy-safe publishing checklist}

Reused from
\hyperref[testing-privacy-safe-publishing-and-the-shiny-demo]{dash05}
for your reference:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  No row-level data in screenshots, AI chats, or public README.
\item
  No small-cell interpretations.
\item
  Descriptive limits stated; no causal language; no national-prevalence
  language.
\item
  Cached classroom data kept separate from any raw retrieval workflow.
\item
  Row-level data never uploaded to external AI tools.
\end{itemize}

\part{Week 9: Communication of Scientific Findings}

\chapter*{Week 9: Communication of Scientific
Findings}\label{week-9-communication-of-scientific-findings-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Week 9: Communication of Scientific
Findings}

\markboth{Week 9: Communication of Scientific Findings}{Week 9:
Communication of Scientific Findings}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

Science communication means explaining scientific evidence clearly and
responsibly for a specific audience. In this course, that means turning
one audited health data finding into a short, plain-language message
that includes evidence, limitations, and appropriate caution.

This week turns one audited result into a short scientific communication
product. The goal is not to make a slide deck bigger. The goal is to
make one claim easier to understand, easier to verify, and harder to
overstate.

You will reuse work you already have: a corrected plot from Assignment
5, a Table 1 or methods note from Assignment 6, and a dashboard
adaptation or limitation statement from Assignment 7.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Required / Draft / Optional This Week}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Required (graded):} the
  \href{../../assignments/assignment08-communication/README.md}{Assignment
  8} files, explained in
  \href{comm05-assignment-a8-and-reference.qmd}{comm05}, plus the
  peer-feedback exchange in \href{comm04-peer-feedback.qmd}{comm04}.
\item
  \textbf{Draft (class work that feeds A8, not graded on its own):} the
  claim-evidence-limitation table and the 3-5 slide draft from the class
  plan below.
\item
  \textbf{Optional (no penalty for skipping):} extra slides beyond the
  3-5 slide story --- the deck and the outline are graded identically,
  so do not pad.
\end{itemize}

Tags like \texttt{{[}required{]}} and \texttt{{[}optional{]}} are
defined in \hyperref[how-to-read-submission-lists]{How To Read
Submission Lists}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Objectives}\label{objectives-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Objectives}

\markright{Objectives}

By the end of the week you can:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  identify an audience and decision context for one result;
\item
  write a claim that includes evidence and a visible limitation;
\item
  build or outline a short Quarto revealjs slide story;
\item
  cite the data source for a figure, table, or dashboard screenshot;
\item
  use peer feedback to improve clarity without inflating the claim.
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Terms This Week}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Knowledge translation (KT):} turning evidence into a form that
  helps a specific person understand it, question it, or act on it.
\item
  \textbf{Decision context:} the decision, conversation, or question
  your audience is facing when they see your result.
\item
  \textbf{Audience-facing text:} writing meant for your audience to read
  directly --- not notes for yourself or your team.
\item
  \textbf{Claim-evidence-limitation:} the three-part pattern used for
  every message in this course: what you say, what supports it, and what
  it cannot prove.
\item
  \textbf{Plain-language takeaway:} the one sentence a non-expert should
  remember, written without jargon.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 8 built a small dashboard-style KT product. Week 9 turns one
dashboard finding, plot, or table into a concise slide story. Week 10
then expands the same evidence into a reproducible Quarto report.

\chapter*{Why Communication Is KT}\label{why-communication-is-kt}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Why Communication Is KT}

\markboth{Why Communication Is KT}{Why Communication Is KT}

\section*{Where This Fits}\label{where-this-fits-18}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where This Fits}

\markright{Where This Fits}

You already have descriptive evidence. A plot, a Table 1, and a
dashboard prototype can all be technically correct and still fail if the
intended audience cannot tell what matters.

Science communication means explaining scientific evidence clearly and
responsibly for a specific audience. In this course, that means turning
one audited health data finding into a short, plain-language message
that includes evidence, limitations, and appropriate caution.

Communication is part of knowledge translation. It turns evidence into a
form that helps a person make sense of a question, ask a better
follow-up, or avoid a bad interpretation.

\section*{The Week 9 Task}\label{the-week-9-task}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Week 9 Task}

\markright{The Week 9 Task}

This week asks for one small communication product:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  one audience;
\item
  one question;
\item
  one figure, table, or dashboard output;
\item
  one plain-language takeaway;
\item
  one limitation;
\item
  one source note.
\end{itemize}

That is enough. More slides are not automatically better.

\section*{Match the Message to the
Audience}\label{match-the-message-to-the-audience}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Match the Message to the Audience}

\markright{Match the Message to the Audience}

Before writing, identify your audience: experts may need methods,
policymakers may need decision relevance, patients may need clear
practical meaning, and the public may need plain language and careful
context.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Audience
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
What they usually need
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
What that means for your message
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Experts (analysts, researchers) & Methods and definitions & Name the
data source, the variables, and how groups were formed \\
Policymakers & Decision relevance & Lead with the takeaway and say what
decision or discussion it informs \\
Patients & Clear practical meaning & Explain what the finding means in
everyday terms, without causing alarm \\
General public & Plain language and careful context & Avoid jargon and
keep the limitation right next to the claim \\
\end{longtable}

The finding and the evidence stay the same. What changes is the
emphasis, the wording, and how much technical detail you show.

\section*{What Counts as Evidence}\label{what-counts-as-evidence}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What Counts as Evidence}

\markright{What Counts as Evidence}

Use work from earlier weeks:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Earlier artifact & How it can support Week 9 \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A5 corrected plot & Evidence slide or visual example \\
A5 caption & Source, statistic, exclusion, and limitation wording \\
A6 Table 1 & Descriptive context or supporting table \\
A6 methods note & Data and cohort explanation \\
A6 provenance note & Source and stewardship note \\
A7 dashboard adaptation & Audience and KT purpose \\
A7 limitation/privacy check & Caution slide or speaker note \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Keep the Claim Small}\label{keep-the-claim-small}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Keep the Claim Small}

\markright{Keep the Claim Small}

Good Week 9 claims sound like this:

\begin{quote}
In this prepared classroom dataset, mean BMI differs across income
groups, but the comparison is descriptive and unweighted.
\end{quote}

Weak Week 9 claims sound like this:

\begin{quote}
Income causes BMI differences.
\end{quote}

The second claim is not supported by the course workflow. The first
claim is cautious, understandable, and grounded in the evidence students
have actually produced.

\section*{AI Use}\label{ai-use}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{AI Use}

\markright{AI Use}

AI may help simplify wording, draft speaker notes, or suggest slide
order. It must not invent sources, make results stronger than the code
supports, or remove limitations to make a message sound cleaner.

\section*{Check Yourself}\label{check-yourself}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Check Yourself}

\markright{Check Yourself}

Before moving on, answer:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Who is the audience?
\item
  What decision, conversation, or question does the communication
  support?
\item
  Which prior artifact is the evidence?
\item
  What limitation must remain visible?
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Claim, Evidence, Limitation}\label{claim-evidence-limitation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Claim, Evidence, Limitation}

\markboth{Claim, Evidence, Limitation}{Claim, Evidence, Limitation}

\section*{The Core Pattern}\label{the-core-pattern}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Core Pattern}

\markright{The Core Pattern}

Every scientific communication product in this course should keep three
pieces together:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Claim:} what you want the audience to understand.
\item
  \textbf{Evidence:} the plot, table, dashboard output, code result, or
  citation that supports it.
\item
  \textbf{Limitation:} what the evidence cannot prove.
\end{enumerate}

If one piece is missing, the message is either hard to trust or too easy
to overstate.

\section*{Build the Table First}\label{build-the-table-first}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Build the Table First}

\markright{Build the Table First}

Before opening revealjs, make a small table.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Piece
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Draft
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Audience & Public-health analyst preparing a briefing \\
Question & How does mean BMI vary by income group in the classroom
NHANES dataset? \\
Claim & Mean BMI differs across income groups in the prepared
dataset. \\
Evidence & A5 corrected plot and A6 Table 1 \\
Limitation & Descriptive, unweighted, classroom snapshot; not causal or
population-weighted \\
Source note & CDC/NCHS NHANES public-use data, prepared for classroom
use \\
\end{longtable}

This table is not an extra deliverable. It is a drafting tool for A8.

\section*{Reuse Earlier Work}\label{reuse-earlier-work}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Reuse Earlier Work}

\markright{Reuse Earlier Work}

\subsection*{From A5}\label{from-a5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{From A5}

Use the corrected plot and caption. The caption already names the
statistic, source, exclusions, and limitation. Shorten it for a slide,
but do not remove the limitation.

\subsection*{From A6}\label{from-a6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{From A6}

Use the Table 1, methods note, and provenance/stewardship note. They
help the audience understand who is included, which values are missing,
and why the result is descriptive.

\subsection*{From A7}\label{from-a7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{From A7}

Use the audience note and limitation/privacy check. They remind you that
a KT product is designed for someone, not for everyone.

\section*{Plain-Language Revision}\label{plain-language-revision}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Plain-Language Revision}

\markright{Plain-Language Revision}

Technical draft:

\begin{quote}
The grouped BMI means show heterogeneity across IncomeGroup strata.
\end{quote}

Plain-language revision:

\begin{quote}
Average BMI is not the same across income groups in this classroom
dataset.
\end{quote}

Limitation sentence:

\begin{quote}
This is a descriptive, unweighted comparison and does not show that
income causes BMI differences.
\end{quote}

Here is a second example, this time about missing data.

Technical draft:

\begin{quote}
Rows with missing BMI values were excluded via
\texttt{filter(!is.na(BMI))} prior to aggregation.
\end{quote}

Plain-language revision:

\begin{quote}
Before calculating the group averages, we left out people whose BMI was
not recorded.
\end{quote}

Limitation sentence:

\begin{quote}
The averages describe only people with a recorded BMI, so groups with
more missing values are less fully represented.
\end{quote}

\section*{What Not to Do}\label{what-not-to-do}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What Not to Do}

\markright{What Not to Do}

Avoid these common shortcuts:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  turning a descriptive pattern into a causal statement;
\item
  hiding the limitation in tiny text;
\item
  showing a figure without naming the data source;
\item
  copying a dashboard screenshot that exposes row-level data;
\item
  asking AI to ``make it more persuasive'' without checking accuracy.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Slide-Level Check}\label{slide-level-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Slide-Level Check}

\markright{Slide-Level Check}

For each slide, ask:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  What is the one claim?
\item
  Where is the evidence?
\item
  Where is the limitation?
\item
  Is the source visible or easy to find?
\end{itemize}

If a peer cannot answer those questions, revise before submission.

\chapter*{Revealjs Starter}\label{revealjs-starter}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Revealjs Starter}

\markboth{Revealjs Starter}{Revealjs Starter}

\section*{Where This Fits}\label{where-this-fits-19}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where This Fits}

\markright{Where This Fits}

This page explains how to use the slide starter shipped in your personal
workspace at
\texttt{assignments/assignment08-communication/starter-slides.qmd}. The
copy in this course book is a reference; do not assume it appears in the
same Explorer as your personal work.

You may also submit a structured slide outline for A8. Both options are
graded identically --- pick the format that fits where you are. The
revealjs path is recommended if you want practice with Quarto slides
before M5.

\section*{Copy, Then Render}\label{copy-then-render}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Copy, Then Render}

\markright{Copy, Then Render}

The workspace starter is configured for its submitted location. Copy and
rename it first using the steps below, then render the submitted
\texttt{slides.qmd}; do not render or edit the untouched template in
place.

\section*{Copy the Starter into Your A8
Folder}\label{copy-the-starter-into-your-a8-folder}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Copy the Starter into Your A8 Folder}

\markright{Copy the Starter into Your A8 Folder}

The starter stays in the weeks folder as a template. Your graded files
belong in the assignment submission folder.

Before editing, copy
\texttt{assignments/assignment08-communication/starter-slides.qmd}
within your personal workspace into
\texttt{assignments/assignment08-communication/submission/} and rename
it \texttt{slides.qmd}. Render from the repository root so the required
\texttt{slides.qmd} and \texttt{slides.html} are created in the correct
folder.

Step by step in VS Code:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  In your personal workspace Explorer, open
  \texttt{assignments/assignment08-communication/}, right-click
  \texttt{starter-slides.qmd}, and choose \textbf{Copy}.
\item
  Right-click the
  \texttt{assignments/assignment08-communication/submission/} folder and
  choose \textbf{Paste}.
\item
  Right-click the pasted copy, choose \textbf{Rename}, and type
  \texttt{slides.qmd}.
\item
  Edit \texttt{slides.qmd} there, then render it by name:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\ExtensionTok{quarto}\NormalTok{ render assignments/assignment08{-}communication/submission/slides.qmd}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you choose the outline path instead, name your file
\texttt{slide-outline.qmd} in the same submission folder.

\section*{The Three-Slide Story}\label{the-three-slide-story}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Three-Slide Story}

\markright{The Three-Slide Story}

The starter uses three content slides:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Question and audience:} who needs the result and why.
\item
  \textbf{Evidence:} one plot, table, or dashboard output.
\item
  \textbf{Limitation and next step:} what the result does not prove and
  what the audience should do next.
\end{enumerate}

For A8, 3-5 slides is enough. A short, honest deck is better than a long
deck full of weak claims.

\section*{Add Evidence}\label{add-evidence}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Add Evidence}

\markright{Add Evidence}

You can add evidence in three ways:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  insert a figure exported from A5;
\item
  copy a small table from A6;
\item
  include a screenshot or preview of the A7 dashboard output if it does
  not expose row-level data.
\end{itemize}

Use relative paths for images. For example:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\AlertTok{![](../../assignment05{-}visualization/submission/corrected{-}plot.png)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

That example is relative to the A8 \texttt{submission/} folder. If the
path does not work, keep the figure beside your slide deck and use a
local relative path such as:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\AlertTok{![](corrected{-}plot.png)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Add a Source Note}\label{add-a-source-note}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Add a Source Note}

\markright{Add a Source Note}

The starter includes a citation to \texttt{@cdc-nhanes}. Keep a source
note near the evidence slide. If you use your project dataset instead of
the NHANES example, replace the source note with the source that matches
your project.

\section*{Speaker Notes}\label{speaker-notes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Speaker Notes}

\markright{Speaker Notes}

Speaker notes are useful, but they are not a place to hide essential
limitations. Put the key limitation on the slide or in a visible
caption. Use notes for backup details such as cohort definition, package
versions, or AI-use checks.

\section*{Before Submitting}\label{before-submitting}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Before Submitting}

\markright{Before Submitting}

Check that:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the deck or outline renders;
\item
  the main evidence is visible;
\item
  the limitation is not hidden;
\item
  the source note is present;
\item
  the AI-use note says what AI helped with and what you checked.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Peer Feedback}\label{peer-feedback}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Peer Feedback}

\markboth{Peer Feedback}{Peer Feedback}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-10}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Peer feedback helps you test whether a scientific message is clear,
grounded, and honest before it reaches the final presentation or
portfolio.

In Week 9, feedback is not about personal preference. It is about
whether the audience can see the claim, evidence, limitation, and
source.

\section*{How and When to Exchange
Feedback}\label{how-and-when-to-exchange-feedback}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{How and When to Exchange Feedback}

\markright{How and When to Exchange Feedback}

Use the in-studio partner review as the pre-submission feedback source
for A8. The Canvas Discussion exchange closes Monday after the Week 9
class at 4:00 PM, the same deadline as A8, and can support later
presentation-deck or portfolio revision.

Use both channels:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{In-studio partner review (during the Week 9 class):} show your
  draft to a partner, complete the feedback table, and make at least one
  documented A8 revision before submitting.
\item
  \textbf{Canvas Discussion (by the standard deadline above):} post your
  plain-language summary and rendered slides or outline, then complete
  the feedback table for at least one classmate. Feedback arriving at
  the deadline is for later revision, not the required pre-submission A8
  change.
\end{enumerate}

You do not need to give classmates access to your repository. To share
an in-progress deck, show your screen in studio, or download the
rendered \texttt{slides.html} from the Explorer (right-click the file,
then \textbf{Download}) and attach it to your Canvas Discussion post.

Feedback you receive can also inform revisions to this work for the
final portfolio.

\section*{Three-Slide Structure}\label{three-slide-structure}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Three-Slide Structure}

\markright{Three-Slide Structure}

Use this structure for a revealjs deck or slide outline:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Question and audience:} who needs this finding, and why?
\item
  \textbf{Evidence:} show one plot, table, or dashboard output and state
  the descriptive pattern.
\item
  \textbf{Limitation and action:} name what the data can and cannot
  support, then state the next decision or question.
\end{enumerate}

Use \url{starter-slides.qmd} if you want a renderable revealjs scaffold.

\section*{Plain-Language Check}\label{plain-language-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Plain-Language Check}

\markright{Plain-Language Check}

For each slide, ask:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  What is the one sentence the audience should remember?
\item
  Which word or phrase would confuse someone outside the course?
\item
  What limitation must remain visible to avoid overclaiming?
\item
  Does the source note match the evidence shown?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Peer Feedback Form}\label{peer-feedback-form}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Peer Feedback Form}

\markright{Peer Feedback Form}

Copy this table into your A8 \texttt{peer-feedback.md}.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Prompt & Peer note \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Main message in one sentence & \\
Intended audience & \\
Evidence shown & \\
Limitation or caution shown & \\
Source note or citation shown & \\
Wording that needs plain-language revision & \\
Accessibility issue, if any & \\
One concrete improvement before submission & \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Citation and Accessibility
Checklist}\label{citation-and-accessibility-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Citation and Accessibility Checklist}

\markright{Citation and Accessibility Checklist}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Data source is named.
\item
  Figure or table has a clear title.
\item
  Axes, units, and group labels are readable.
\item
  Color choices work without relying on color alone.
\item
  Slide text is brief enough to read during a talk.
\item
  Limitation is visible.
\item
  AI-assisted wording has been checked against the evidence.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Good Feedback Sounds Like}\label{good-feedback-sounds-like}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Good Feedback Sounds Like}

\markright{Good Feedback Sounds Like}

Helpful:

\begin{quote}
The main claim is clear, but the limitation is only in speaker notes.
Put the unweighted/descriptive caution under the figure.
\end{quote}

Less helpful:

\begin{quote}
Looks good.
\end{quote}

\section*{Student Output}\label{student-output-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Student Output}

\markright{Student Output}

Submit the rendered slide deck or slide outline, completed peer feedback
form, plain-language summary, source note, and AI-use note required by
\href{../../assignments/assignment08-communication/README.md}{Assignment
8}.

\chapter*{Assignment 8 Walkthrough}\label{assignment-8-walkthrough}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Assignment 8 Walkthrough}

\markboth{Assignment 8 Walkthrough}{Assignment 8 Walkthrough}

\section*{Where This Fits}\label{where-this-fits-20}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where This Fits}

\markright{Where This Fits}

This page connects the Week 9 studio work to
\href{../../assignments/assignment08-communication/README.md}{Assignment
8}. It does not add new deliverables. It explains what each required
file should contain and how to check it before submitting.

Assignment 8 is due the Monday after Week 9 class, 4 PM. It also gives
you reusable material for the final portfolio's narrated presentation
deck.

\section*{The Six A8 Files}\label{the-six-a8-files}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Six A8 Files}

\markright{The Six A8 Files}

Each file goes in
\texttt{assignments/assignment08-communication/submission/}.

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{1. \texttt{slides.qmd} or
\texttt{slide-outline.qmd}}{1. slides.qmd or slide-outline.qmd}}\label{slides.qmd-or-slide-outline.qmd}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. \texttt{slides.qmd} or
\texttt{slide-outline.qmd}}

Use one of these options:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{slides.qmd}: a revealjs deck based on the personal-workspace
  \texttt{assignments/assignment08-communication/starter-slides.qmd};
\item
  \texttt{slide-outline.qmd}: a structured outline if you are not ready
  to render revealjs.
\end{itemize}

Both options are graded identically --- choose the format that fits
where you are. If you choose the outline for A8, convert the revised
outline into a renderable \texttt{slides.qmd} for the presentation deck.
The outline remains fully eligible for the A8 score; the deck is the
later slide-production stage.

To create \texttt{slides.qmd}, copy the starter within your personal
workspace from \texttt{assignments/assignment08-communication/} into its
\texttt{submission/} folder and rename it before editing. Render from
the personal-workspace root so \texttt{slides.html} is created in the
correct place. Step-by-step instructions are in
\hyperref[revealjs-starter]{comm03}.

Include 3-5 slides or sections: context, evidence, takeaway, limitation,
and next step.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} the source file renders or is readable as a complete
  outline.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{2. \texttt{slides.html} or
\texttt{slide-outline.html}}{2. slides.html or slide-outline.html}}\label{slides.html-or-slide-outline.html}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. \texttt{slides.html} or
\texttt{slide-outline.html}}

This is the rendered output. If you use revealjs, the result should open
in the browser as slides.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} open the rendered file and check that the evidence
  and limitation are visible.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{3.
\texttt{plain-language-summary.md}}{3. plain-language-summary.md}}\label{plain-language-summary.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. \texttt{plain-language-summary.md}}

Write 150-250 words for the intended audience. Use plain language, but
keep the evidence honest.

Include:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  who the audience is;
\item
  what the main result says;
\item
  what the result does not prove;
\item
  what action, question, or conversation should follow.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{4.
\texttt{peer-feedback.md}}{4. peer-feedback.md}}\label{peer-feedback.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. \texttt{peer-feedback.md}}

Use the feedback table from \hyperref[peer-feedback]{comm04}. Include
the feedback you received and one sentence explaining what you changed.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} at least one revision is tied to a peer comment.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{5.
\texttt{source-note.md}}{5. source-note.md}}\label{source-note.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. \texttt{source-note.md}}

Name the dataset or source behind the evidence. For the NHANES classroom
example, the source note can say:

\begin{quote}
Source: prepared classroom snapshot derived from CDC/NCHS NHANES
public-use data. Results are descriptive and unweighted.
\end{quote}

For a term project, replace this with the correct project source.

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{6.
\texttt{ai-use-note.md}}{6. ai-use-note.md}}\label{ai-use-note.md-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. \texttt{ai-use-note.md}}

If AI helped draft, simplify, or reorganize your communication, state:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  what it helped with;
\item
  what you changed;
\item
  how you checked the claim against the evidence.
\end{itemize}

If you did not use AI, write one sentence saying so.

\section*{Reproducibility Check}\label{reproducibility-check-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Reproducibility Check}

\markright{Reproducibility Check}

Before submission:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the deck or outline source is committed;
\item
  the rendered HTML is committed;
\item
  any image paths are relative;
\item
  the evidence can be traced to A5, A6, A7, or project files;
\item
  the source note and limitation are visible;
\item
  the AI-use note is specific.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Accessibility Check}\label{accessibility-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Accessibility Check}

\markright{Accessibility Check}

Slides should be readable in a live presentation. Check:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  titles are short;
\item
  body text is large enough;
\item
  figure labels are readable;
\item
  color is not the only way to distinguish groups;
\item
  the final slide gives the audience a clear next step.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Where This Goes Next}\label{where-this-goes-next-21}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where This Goes Next}

\markright{Where This Goes Next}

Week 10 expands the same communication discipline into a report: the
claim becomes a results paragraph, the evidence becomes a code-generated
table or figure, and the limitation becomes part of the written
interpretation.

\part{Week 10: Writing \& Publishing Reports}

\chapter*{Week 10: Writing \& Publishing
Reports}\label{week-10-writing-publishing-reports-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Week 10: Writing \& Publishing Reports}

\markboth{Week 10: Writing \& Publishing Reports}{Week 10: Writing \&
Publishing Reports}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

This week turns a slide-sized finding into a short reproducible report.
A report does more than present a result. It shows where the data came
from, how the output was generated, what the evidence supports, and what
remains uncertain.

The default publishing pathway is a private preview unless the data,
repository, and group privacy checks show that public sharing is safe.

If you want the big picture first, the
\href{report04-private-preview.qmd}{Private Preview page} opens with a
workflow diagram showing how editing, rendering, previewing, committing,
and publishing fit together.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Required / Draft / Optional This Week}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Required (graded):} the
  \href{../../assignments/assignment09-reporting/README.md}{Assignment
  9} files and the group
  \href{../../milestones/m3-project-update/README.md}{M3 project
  update}, explained in
  \href{report05-assignment-a9-and-m3.qmd}{report05}.
\item
  \textbf{Draft (class work that feeds A9/M3, not graded on its own):}
  the report outline, rendered template, and grounding audit from the
  class plan below.
\item
  \textbf{Optional (no penalty for skipping):} public publishing --- the
  course default is a private preview
  (\href{report04-private-preview.qmd}{report04}).
\end{itemize}

Tags like \texttt{{[}required{]}} and \texttt{{[}optional{]}} are
defined in \hyperref[how-to-read-submission-lists]{How To Read
Submission Lists}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Objectives}\label{objectives-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Objectives}

\markright{Objectives}

By the end of the week you can:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  structure a short Quarto report around one descriptive question;
\item
  generate one table and one figure from code;
\item
  cite a data source using a BibTeX workflow;
\item
  trace key claims to code output, citations, or limitations;
\item
  choose a private or public preview route based on privacy and
  stewardship checks.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 9 turned one finding into a concise slide story. Week 10 expands
the same evidence into a reproducible report and M3 project update. Week
11 then stress-tests whether the report and KT product run in a fresh
environment.

\section*{Dashboard Case Study: NHANES
Equity}\label{dashboard-case-study-nhanes-equity-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Dashboard Case Study: NHANES Equity}

\markright{Dashboard Case Study: NHANES Equity}

The \textbf{NHANES Health Equity Dashboard} is now the foreground case
study. Use it as a small knowledge-translation product built on the
cached NHANES equity dataset.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  App code: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/app/app.R}
\item
  Cached data:
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.rds} and
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Optional rebuild script:
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/scripts/build\_nhanes\_equity.R}
\end{itemize}

Run the dashboard from the repository root:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{shiny}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{runApp}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/app"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The app is offline-first: it loads the local RDS cache when available.
Rebuilding from CDC uses \texttt{nhanesA}, requires internet access, and
is optional unless assigned.

Companion materials:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Week 10 report template:
  \texttt{weeks/week10-reporting-publishing/report-template.qmd}
\item
  Week 11 static dashboard demo:
  \texttt{weeks/week11-portfolio-surgery/static-dashboard-ojs.qmd}
\end{itemize}

\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Reading Order}

\markright{Reading Order}

\section*{Class Plan}\label{class-plan-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Class Plan}

\markright{Class Plan}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Start from one Week 9 claim or one M3-ready finding.
\item
  Draft a report outline: question, data, methods, results, limitations.
\item
  Render the \href{report-template.qmd}{Report Template} and identify
  the table, figure, citation, and limitation.
\item
  Add or revise one citation/source note.
\item
  Complete a grounding audit for three claims.
\item
  Decide whether the preview should stay private or can be public-safe.
\end{enumerate}

For step 3, always name the file you are rendering. In your personal
workspace, use:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\ExtensionTok{quarto}\NormalTok{ render assignments/assignment09{-}reporting/submission/report.qmd}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{How to render: the course default}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Open the \texttt{.qmd} file you want to render, then open the
  \textbf{Command Palette} (\textbf{View → Command Palette}, or press
  \textbf{Ctrl+Shift+P} on Windows/Linux / \textbf{Cmd+Shift+P} on Mac)
  and run \textbf{Quarto: Render Document}. The Quarto extension renders
  the file and opens a preview pane.
\item
  \textbf{Always-works fallback (terminal):} type the render command
  with the file's path as shown in the Explorer, for example:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\ExtensionTok{quarto}\NormalTok{ render practice\_report.qmd}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

  Always name the file --- a bare \texttt{quarto\ render} rebuilds the
  \emph{whole} project and takes much longer.
\item
  If you see a \textbf{Render} (or \textbf{Preview}) button in the
  editor toolbar, it does the same thing as step 1.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Where did the output go?} Watch the render log for the
\texttt{Output\ created:} line --- it names the exact \texttt{.html}
file created. By default the file appears next to your \texttt{.qmd}; in
projects that set an output directory (like this book's \texttt{docs/}
folder), it appears there instead. If no preview opened automatically,
find that \texttt{.html} file in the Explorer, right-click it, and
choose \textbf{Download} to open it in your browser (or \textbf{Open
Preview} if available).

\end{tcolorbox}

M3 feedback request.

\end{itemize}

\section*{Definition of Done}\label{definition-of-done-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definition of Done}

\markright{Definition of Done}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The report source renders.
\item
  One table and one figure are generated by code.
\item
  Citations resolve.
\item
  The limitation section avoids unsupported causal or population-level
  claims.
\item
  The preview route protects data and group privacy.
\end{itemize}

\section*{What Students Leave With}\label{what-students-leave-with-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What Students Leave With}

\markright{What Students Leave With}

A short report draft and project update that can be reviewed in Week 11
for reproducibility, clarity, auditability, and KT value.

\chapter*{Report Goals}\label{report-goals}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Report Goals}

\markboth{Report Goals}{Report Goals}

\section*{Where This Fits}\label{where-this-fits-21}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where This Fits}

\markright{Where This Fits}

Slides help an audience understand one message quickly. Dashboards help
an audience explore one question. Reports show the audit trail: data,
methods, code-generated evidence, interpretation, limitations,
citations, and reproducibility.

Week 10 asks you to make that audit trail visible.

\section*{Who Is the Report For?}\label{who-is-the-report-for}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Who Is the Report For?}

\markright{Who Is the Report For?}

Write the main report for a non-technical decision-maker: someone who
could act on your finding but does not read code. Keep the body in plain
language --- question, evidence, what it means, what it does not prove.
Put technical material such as code detail, package notes, and render
instructions in a technical appendix at the end. This is the same
audience as your A8 slides, so the two should feel consistent.

\section*{The Report Is Not Bigger
Slides}\label{the-report-is-not-bigger-slides}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Report Is Not Bigger Slides}

\markright{The Report Is Not Bigger Slides}

A report should answer:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  What question is being asked?
\item
  Which data support the answer?
\item
  What was included or excluded?
\item
  Which outputs were generated by code?
\item
  What does the result mean?
\item
  What does the result not prove?
\item
  How can another person rerun or inspect it?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Reuse Earlier Work}\label{reuse-earlier-work-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Reuse Earlier Work}

\markright{Reuse Earlier Work}

The report should reuse artifacts instead of creating a new analysis
from scratch.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Earlier artifact & Report role \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A5 corrected plot & Figure \\
A5 caption & Figure caption and limitation \\
A6 Table 1 & Descriptive table \\
A6 methods note & Methods paragraph \\
A6 provenance note & Data source paragraph \\
A7 dashboard note & Audience and KT context \\
A8 slide claim & Results paragraph \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Keep the Scope Descriptive}\label{keep-the-scope-descriptive}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Keep the Scope Descriptive}

\markright{Keep the Scope Descriptive}

The term project does not require formal modelling, prediction, causal
inference, or hypothesis testing. A strong report can be descriptive if
it is transparent, reproducible, and useful for the intended audience.

\section*{Report and M3}\label{report-and-m3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Report and M3}

\markright{Report and M3}

M3 is a project update, not a final report. The draft should show:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  what evidence is ready;
\item
  what still needs feedback;
\item
  how the dashboard-style product connects to the report;
\item
  what reproducibility risks remain.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Check Yourself}\label{check-yourself-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Check Yourself}

\markright{Check Yourself}

Before writing paragraphs, write one sentence:

\begin{quote}
This report answers \_\_\_\_\_\_ for \_\_\_\_\_\_ using \_\_\_\_\_\_,
and the main limitation is \_\_\_\_\_\_.
\end{quote}

If that sentence is hard to finish, narrow the report question.

\chapter*{Report Structure}\label{report-structure}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Report Structure}

\markboth{Report Structure}{Report Structure}

\section*{A Beginner-Friendly
Structure}\label{a-beginner-friendly-structure}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A Beginner-Friendly Structure}

\markright{A Beginner-Friendly Structure}

Use this structure for A9 and M3 unless your project clearly needs a
small adaptation:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Question
\item
  Data source
\item
  Methods
\item
  Results
\item
  Limitations
\item
  Reproducibility and AI-use note
\end{enumerate}

The structure is simple on purpose. It gives readers enough information
to understand, check, and reuse the work.

\section*{Question}\label{question-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Question}

\markright{Question}

A good report question is specific:

\begin{quote}
How does mean BMI vary by income group among adults age 20-80 in the
prepared NHANES classroom dataset?
\end{quote}

Avoid broad questions such as:

\begin{quote}
What affects health?
\end{quote}

\section*{Data Source}\label{data-source}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Data Source}

\markright{Data Source}

Name:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the original data source;
\item
  the version or snapshot used;
\item
  the access route;
\item
  restrictions or stewardship cautions.
\end{itemize}

Use your A6 provenance/stewardship note as the starting point.

\section*{Methods}\label{methods}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Methods}

\markright{Methods}

State choices before showing results:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  cohort or inclusion criteria;
\item
  variables used;
\item
  missingness handling;
\item
  whether the summary is descriptive, unweighted, weighted, predictive,
  or causal.
\end{itemize}

For this course, most reports should be descriptive unless the
instructor explicitly approved another approach.

\section*{Results}\label{results}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Results}

\markright{Results}

Include at least:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  one code-generated table;
\item
  one code-generated figure;
\item
  a short paragraph that states the pattern without overstating it.
\end{itemize}

Do not paste screenshots when code can generate the output.

\section*{Limitations}\label{limitations}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Limitations}

\markright{Limitations}

Limitations belong in the report, not only in an appendix. Include
limitations that affect interpretation, such as unweighted summaries,
missing data, prepared classroom data, small cells, or restricted public
sharing.

\section*{Reproducibility and AI Use}\label{reproducibility-and-ai-use}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Reproducibility and AI Use}

\markright{Reproducibility and AI Use}

End with enough information for a reviewer:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  how the report was rendered;
\item
  where data came from;
\item
  where package/dependency notes live;
\item
  what AI helped with;
\item
  how AI-assisted content was checked.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Report Template: NHANES Equity
Summary}\label{report-template-nhanes-equity-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Report Template: NHANES Equity Summary}

\markboth{Report Template: NHANES Equity Summary}{Report Template:
NHANES Equity Summary}

\section*{Standalone YAML}\label{standalone-yaml}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Standalone YAML}

\markright{Standalone YAML}

If you copy this template into a project report outside the book, start
the file with this YAML header:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\PreprocessorTok{{-}{-}{-}}
\FunctionTok{title}\KeywordTok{:}\AttributeTok{ }\StringTok{"NHANES Equity Summary"}
\FunctionTok{format}\KeywordTok{:}\AttributeTok{ html}
\FunctionTok{bibliography}\KeywordTok{:}\AttributeTok{ references.bib}
\PreprocessorTok{{-}{-}{-}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

This runnable template shows how to pair a dashboard with a short
reproducible report. The dashboard supports exploration; the report
fixes one question, documents the data, and cites the source.

\section*{Question}\label{question-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Question}

\markright{Question}

How does mean BMI vary by income group among adults age 20-80 in the
prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom dataset?

\section*{Data Source}\label{data-source-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Data Source}

\markright{Data Source}

The report uses the cached CSV snapshot at
\texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}. The source
data are derived from CDC/NCHS NHANES public-use files (Centers for
Disease Control and Prevention, National Center for Health Statistics
2026).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tidyverse)  }\CommentTok{\# AI{-}EDIT(2026{-}06{-}23): tidyverse{-}default — consolidated core library() calls into library(tidyverse)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(knitr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(data\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Methods}\label{methods-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Methods}

\markright{Methods}

We restricted the classroom dataset to adults age 20-80 and summarized
BMI by income group. The summaries are unweighted and descriptive. They
should not be interpreted as causal effects or national population
estimates.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{analysis\_df }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{missingness }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ analysis\_df }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{rows =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_income =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_age =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(Age))}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(missingness, }\AttributeTok{caption =} \StringTok{"Missingness check for key report variables."}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}rrrr@{}}
\caption{Missingness check for key report variables.}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
rows & missing\_bmi & missing\_income & missing\_age \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
rows & missing\_bmi & missing\_income & missing\_age \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
57137 & 1009 & 5395 & 0 \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Results}\label{results-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Results}

\markright{Results}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{income\_summary }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ analysis\_df }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \AttributeTok{sd\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{BMI, mean (SD)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"\%.1f (\%.1f)"}\NormalTok{, mean\_bmi, sd\_bmi)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup, n, }\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{BMI, mean (SD)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(income\_summary, }\AttributeTok{caption =} \StringTok{"Descriptive BMI summary by income group."}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrl@{}}
\caption{Descriptive BMI summary by income group.}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
IncomeGroup & n & BMI, mean (SD) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead

IncomeGroup & n & BMI, mean (SD) \\

\endlastfoot

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{plot\_df }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ analysis\_df }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(plot\_df, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ mean\_bmi)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_col}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"\#2C7FB8"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_text}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{label =} \FunctionTok{paste0}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"n="}\NormalTok{, n)), }\AttributeTok{vjust =} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{size =} \FloatTok{3.5}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI by income group"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"Adults age 20{-}80; unweighted descriptive summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"Income group"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{expand\_limits}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{base\_size =} \DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{)}

}

NHANES Health Equity classroom dataset. Values are unweighted and

\section*{Limitations}\label{limitations-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Limitations}

\markright{Limitations}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The table and figure use a prepared classroom dataset rather than raw
  NHANES files.
\item
  The summaries are unweighted.
\item
  The comparison is descriptive and does not establish causality.
\item
  Missing BMI or income group values are excluded from the grouped
  summary.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Plain-Language Summary}\label{plain-language-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Plain-Language Summary}

\markright{Plain-Language Summary}

In this classroom dataset, mean BMI differs across income groups. The
result is useful for practicing reproducible reporting and dashboard
interpretation, but it should be described as a descriptive pattern
rather than a causal finding.

\section*{AI-Use and Audit Note}\label{ai-use-and-audit-note}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{AI-Use and Audit Note}

\markright{AI-Use and Audit Note}

Document any AI assistance used to draft code, captions, or
interpretation. State exactly which output you checked against the code,
data dictionary, or rendered report.

\section*{Publishing Checklist}\label{publishing-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Publishing Checklist}

\markright{Publishing Checklist}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Report renders from source.
\item
  Citations resolve.
\item
  Figures and tables are generated by code.
\item
  Private preview is used unless the data, repository, and group privacy
  checks support public sharing.
\item
  Public preview contains no restricted data, private links,
  credentials, or sensitive row-level examples.
\item
  Staged files were reviewed before committing: no raw row-level data,
  credentials, \texttt{.RData} workspaces, or stray generated files, and
  \texttt{.gitignore} covers the data folder and rendered outputs ---
  see the \href{report04-private-preview.qmd}{pre-commit privacy
  checklist}.
\item
  Private repository access is granted to course staff when needed.
\item
  Canvas submission includes the repository link, report path, and
  latest commit hash.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Grounding Audit Prompt}\label{grounding-audit-prompt}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Grounding Audit Prompt}

\markright{Grounding Audit Prompt}

Select three claims from the report. For each claim, write the
supporting evidence source: code chunk, table, figure, or citation.
Revise any claim that cannot be traced to evidence in the report.

\chapter*{Citations and Grounding}\label{citations-and-grounding}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Citations and Grounding}

\markboth{Citations and Grounding}{Citations and Grounding}

\section*{Why This Matters}\label{why-this-matters-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why This Matters}

\markright{Why This Matters}

Reports can sound confident even when claims are weakly supported. A
grounding audit forces each important claim to point back to a source:
code output, a table, a figure, a citation, or a stated limitation.

\section*{Citation Workflow}\label{citation-workflow}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Citation Workflow}

\markright{Citation Workflow}

The course uses BibTeX references through \texttt{references.bib}. A
report YAML header can include:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\PreprocessorTok{{-}{-}{-}}
\FunctionTok{title}\KeywordTok{:}\AttributeTok{ }\StringTok{"Project Report"}
\FunctionTok{format}\KeywordTok{:}\AttributeTok{ html}
\FunctionTok{bibliography}\KeywordTok{:}\AttributeTok{ ../../references.bib}
\PreprocessorTok{{-}{-}{-}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Then cite a source in text:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{The NHANES classroom example is derived from CDC/NCHS NHANES public{-}use data }\CommentTok{[}\OtherTok{@cdc{-}nhanes}\CommentTok{]}\NormalTok{.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If your project uses a different source, add the relevant bibliography
entry to the report's local \texttt{references.bib} or to the project
bibliography.

\section*{Grounding Audit}\label{grounding-audit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Grounding Audit}

\markright{Grounding Audit}

For A9, choose at least three claims and trace them.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Claim
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Evidence type
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Evidence location
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Decision
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Mean BMI differs by income group. & Figure/table & Results plot and
summary table & Keep, with descriptive wording \\
Income causes BMI differences. & None & Not supported & Remove or
rewrite \\
The summary is unweighted. & Methods/code & Methods paragraph and code &
Keep \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{What Counts as Evidence}\label{what-counts-as-evidence-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What Counts as Evidence}

\markright{What Counts as Evidence}

Evidence can be:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a code-generated table;
\item
  a code-generated figure;
\item
  a data source citation;
\item
  a methods paragraph;
\item
  a limitation statement;
\item
  a reproducibility log.
\end{itemize}

Evidence is not:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  an AI-generated sentence without verification;
\item
  a visual impression with no source;
\item
  a claim copied from a website without citation;
\item
  a dashboard interaction that cannot be reproduced.
\end{itemize}

\section*{AI-Use Check}\label{ai-use-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{AI-Use Check}

\markright{AI-Use Check}

If AI helped draft report prose, audit the generated text for:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  invented sources;
\item
  stronger claims than the code supports;
\item
  causal language;
\item
  missing limitations;
\item
  mismatched variable names or statistics.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Before Moving On}\label{before-moving-on}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Before Moving On}

\markright{Before Moving On}

If a claim cannot be grounded, do one of three things:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  add the missing evidence;
\item
  soften the wording;
\item
  remove the claim.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Private Preview}\label{private-preview}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Private Preview}

\markboth{Private Preview}{Private Preview}

\section*{How the Pieces Fit
Together}\label{how-the-pieces-fit-together}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{How the Pieces Fit Together}

\markright{How the Pieces Fit Together}

Quarto turns your source file into HTML. GitHub stores both. The private
preview route stops there: course staff open the committed HTML or
render the report in a fresh Codespace. GitHub Pages is an optional last
step that turns the rendered HTML into a public website.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{weeks/week10-reporting-publishing/../../assets/images/week10/private-preview-workflow.png}}

}

\caption{The publishing workflow: edit \texttt{report.qmd}, render with
Quarto, preview the HTML, then commit and sync. A privacy check routes
to a private preview by default, or optionally to a public GitHub Pages
site.}

\end{figure}%

\section*{Default Path}\label{default-path}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Default Path}

\markright{Default Path}

For this course, publishing usually means creating a rendered, shareable
output for grading or private review. Public publishing is optional and
should only happen after privacy and safety checks.

The default publishing route is a private preview unless the project
passes privacy and stewardship checks for public sharing.

Private preview means:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the report renders to HTML;
\item
  the rendered file is committed if the assignment asks for it;
\item
  the repository link is submitted on Canvas;
\item
  course staff have access if the repository is private.
\end{itemize}

This is enough for A9 and M3.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Commit required rendered artifacts}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

The submission list is authoritative. If an assignment or milestone
explicitly requires a rendered HTML or PDF, commit it with the source so
staff can inspect the work. Do not add global \texttt{*.html} or
\texttt{*.pdf} patterns to the course workspace \texttt{.gitignore}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Pre-Commit Privacy
Checklist}\label{pre-commit-privacy-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Pre-Commit Privacy Checklist}

\markright{Pre-Commit Privacy Checklist}

Run this check every time, before you commit and sync:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open the Source Control panel and read the list of staged files.
\item
  Unstage anything that should not go online: raw restricted or
  row-level data, credentials or tokens, \texttt{.RData} workspaces, OS
  junk such as \texttt{.DS\_Store}, and unrelated generated files. Keep
  every rendered artifact named in the assessment brief staged.
\item
  Open \texttt{.gitignore} and confirm it covers secrets,
  machine-specific files, and temporary support folders without globally
  excluding required HTML or PDF. If an unwanted file keeps reappearing,
  add a narrow rule for that file type or path.
\item
  Skim the rendered HTML one last time for private URLs, tokens, or
  row-level records in printed output.
\end{enumerate}

If any item fails, fix it before syncing. It is much easier to keep a
file out of Git than to remove it after it has been pushed.

\section*{When Public Publishing Is
Safe}\label{when-public-publishing-is-safe}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{When Public Publishing Is Safe}

\markright{When Public Publishing Is Safe}

GitHub Pages or another public preview is appropriate only when:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the data are public and allowed to be shared;
\item
  no restricted raw data are committed;
\item
  no sensitive row-level records appear in outputs;
\item
  no private URLs, tokens, or credentials appear;
\item
  the group agrees that public sharing fits the project context;
\item
  the README explains data source and sharing limits.
\end{itemize}

If any item is uncertain, keep the preview private.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{GitHub Pages sites are always public}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

A GitHub Pages site is public on the open internet even when the source
repository is private. Turning on Pages publishes the rendered content
for anyone to see. If your project must stay private, the ``private
equivalent'' is course staff previewing in a fresh Codespace or reading
the committed HTML in your private repository --- not a Pages site.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{GitHub Pages as an Optional
Extension}\label{github-pages-as-an-optional-extension}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{GitHub Pages as an Optional Extension}

\markright{GitHub Pages as an Optional Extension}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Demo only --- not graded}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

This pathway is a \textbf{demonstration only}. It is not graded and not
required. Using it for graded work requires \textbf{instructor approval
first}. The supported, graded pathway in this course is the Quarto
workflow.

\end{tcolorbox}

GitHub Pages is useful for public portfolios, but it is not required for
every student project. Public publishing should never outrank privacy,
data-use rules, or stewardship obligations.

If your group uses GitHub Pages, record:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the public URL;
\item
  the commit hash;
\item
  what was checked before publishing;
\item
  what data or outputs are intentionally excluded.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Private Preview Checklist}\label{private-preview-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Private Preview Checklist}

\markright{Private Preview Checklist}

For the default route, record:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  report path;
\item
  rendered HTML path;
\item
  repository link;
\item
  latest commit hash;
\item
  who has access;
\item
  any files intentionally excluded from public sharing.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Common Errors and Fixes}\label{common-errors-and-fixes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Common Errors and Fixes}

\markright{Common Errors and Fixes}

Most rendering and publishing problems this week have boring causes.
Check this table before asking for help.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
What you see
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Likely cause
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Fix
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{quarto:\ command\ not\ found} & The terminal opened before
Codespace setup finished & Open a new terminal and retry; if it
persists, rebuild the Codespace \\
Render stops with red text naming an R package & The package is not
installed in this environment & Read the first error line for the
package name, then follow the week's setup steps; do not change the
environment beyond that without asking \\
\texttt{cannot\ open\ file} or \texttt{...\ does\ not\ exist} for a CSV
& Absolute path, or wrong working folder & Make the path relative to the
project root and re-render (see the path check on the
\href{report05-assignment-a9-and-m3.qmd}{Assignment 9 and M3 page}) \\
Render finishes but no preview opens & The preview pane did not launch &
Find the \texttt{.html} file named in the \texttt{Output\ created:}
line, right-click it in the Explorer, and choose \textbf{Download} or
\textbf{Open Preview} \\
Citations appear as \texttt{{[}@key{]}} in the output & Bibliography
file not found, or key misspelled & Check the \texttt{bibliography:}
path in the YAML header and the key spelling in
\texttt{references.bib} \\
Pages site shows a 404 or old content & Pages is not enabled, or is
still deploying & Wait a few minutes and refresh; check the repository's
\textbf{Settings → Pages}; remember Pages is optional \\
\end{longtable}

If the fix is not obvious after a careful read of the first error line,
copy the exact error message into your help request.

The same decision returns in the final portfolio. Public repositories
