\section*{A9 Submission Files}\label{a9-submission-files}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A9 Submission Files}

\markright{A9 Submission Files}

Each A9 file goes in
\texttt{assignments/assignment09-reporting/submission/}.

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{report.qmd}}{report.qmd}}\label{report.qmd}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{\texttt{report.qmd}}

A rerunnable Quarto report with question, data, methods, results,
limitations, citations, and AI-use or audit notes.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} render from the repository root or clearly documented
  project folder.
\end{itemize}

To render it, always name the file, for example:
\texttt{quarto\ render\ assignments/assignment09-reporting/submission/report.qmd}.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{How to render: the course default}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Open the \texttt{.qmd} file you want to render, then open the
  \textbf{Command Palette} (\textbf{View → Command Palette}, or press
  \textbf{Ctrl+Shift+P} on Windows/Linux / \textbf{Cmd+Shift+P} on Mac)
  and run \textbf{Quarto: Render Document}. The Quarto extension renders
  the file and opens a preview pane.
\item
  \textbf{Always-works fallback (terminal):} type the render command
  with the file's path as shown in the Explorer, for example:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\ExtensionTok{quarto}\NormalTok{ render practice\_report.qmd}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

  Always name the file --- a bare \texttt{quarto\ render} rebuilds the
  \emph{whole} project and takes much longer.
\item
  If you see a \textbf{Render} (or \textbf{Preview}) button in the
  editor toolbar, it does the same thing as step 1.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Where did the output go?} Watch the render log for the
\texttt{Output\ created:} line --- it names the exact \texttt{.html}
file created. By default the file appears next to your \texttt{.qmd}; in
projects that set an output directory (like this book's \texttt{docs/}
folder), it appears there instead. If no preview opened automatically,
find that \texttt{.html} file in the Explorer, right-click it, and
choose \textbf{Download} to open it in your browser (or \textbf{Open
Preview} if available).

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{report.html}}{report.html}}\label{report.html}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{\texttt{report.html}}

The rendered report.

After rendering, open the HTML file in browser preview. Confirm that the
title, table, figure, citations, limitations, and references appear
correctly before committing your work.

Yes, commit this HTML file. The course rule is simple: when an
assessment explicitly lists a rendered artifact, commit it with its
source.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} table, figure, citations, and limitations appear.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{references.bib}}{references.bib}}\label{references.bib}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{\texttt{references.bib}}

Bibliography entries used by the report. This may be a copied subset of
the project bibliography if that makes the submission easier to inspect.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} citations resolve during render.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{outputs/}}{outputs/}}\label{outputs}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{\texttt{outputs/}}

Use this folder only if your report writes external files such as CSV
tables or exported figures.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} outputs are generated by code, not manually edited.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{publishing-checklist.md}}{publishing-checklist.md}}\label{publishing-checklist.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{\texttt{publishing-checklist.md}}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{grounding-audit.md}}{grounding-audit.md}}\label{grounding-audit.md}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{m3-update-note.md}}{m3-update-note.md}}\label{m3-update-note.md}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ai-use-note.md}}{ai-use-note.md}}\label{ai-use-note.md-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{\texttt{ai-use-note.md}}

\section*{Edit These, Not Those}\label{edit-these-not-those}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Edit These, Not Those}

\markright{Edit These, Not Those}

Edit the source \texttt{.qmd} and supporting text files. Do not manually
edit generated HTML files, cached outputs, raw data, credentials,
\texttt{.RData} workspaces, or system/configuration files unless
instructed.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Edit:} \texttt{report.qmd}, \texttt{references.bib}, and the
  notes (\texttt{publishing-checklist.md}, \texttt{grounding-audit.md},
  \texttt{m3-update-note.md}, \texttt{ai-use-note.md}).
\item
  \textbf{Do not hand-edit:} \texttt{report.html} (re-render instead),
  files in \texttt{outputs/} (regenerate them from code), raw data
  files, and configuration files such as \texttt{\_quarto.yml}.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Feedback Request}\label{feedback-request}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Feedback Request}

\markright{Feedback Request}

A useful M3 feedback request is specific:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Is the main claim supported by the table and figure?
\item
  Is the audience clear?
\item
  Is the limitation strong enough?
\item
  Would the dashboard preview help the intended audience?
\item
  What reproducibility issue should be fixed before Week 11?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Before Submitting}\label{before-submitting-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Before Submitting}

\markright{Before Submitting}

Before rendering for submission, search your code for local paths such
as \texttt{/Users/}, \texttt{C:\textbackslash{}}, \texttt{Downloads}, or
\texttt{Desktop}. Replace them with project-relative paths. The Search
panel (the magnifying glass icon in the left sidebar) checks every file
at once.

Before committing, check the staged files. If you see raw row-level
data, credentials, \texttt{.RData}, OS junk, or generated files that are
not allowed, unstage them and check \texttt{.gitignore} before syncing.
The full \hyperref[private-preview]{pre-commit privacy checklist} is on
the Private Preview page.

Check:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  report renders;
\item
  table and figure are code-generated;
\item
  citations resolve;
\item
  preview route is private unless public-safe;
\item
  M3 feedback request names the decisions that still need review;
\item
  all required files are committed and synced.
\end{itemize}

\part{Week 11: Portfolio Surgery}

\chapter*{Week 11: Portfolio Surgery}\label{week-11-portfolio-surgery-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Week 11: Portfolio Surgery}

\markboth{Week 11: Portfolio Surgery}{Week 11: Portfolio Surgery}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-10}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

This week is about making the project survive review. The goal is not to
add features. The goal is to find and repair the failures that would
stop another person from opening, rendering, understanding, or trusting
the final portfolio.

Portfolio surgery means fixing, simplifying, documenting, and polishing
the work you already have --- not building something new.

The core pathway is a fresh Codespace reproducibility test, README and
path repair, peer review, and final risk list. Advanced deployment
pathways stay optional and demo-only.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Required / Draft / Optional This Week}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Required (graded):} the
  \href{../../milestones/m4-peer-review/README.md}{M4 peer review}.
  Portfolio Surgery itself is an in-class working session (not
  separately graded); its outputs feed M4 and the Final Portfolio, as
  explained in \href{port06-assignment-a10-final-portfolio.qmd}{port06}.
\item
  \textbf{Draft (class work that feeds M4 and the portfolio, not graded
  on its own):} the fresh-test failure log and repair notes from
  \href{port02-fresh-codespace-test.qmd}{port02}.
\item
  \textbf{Optional / demo-only (no penalty for skipping):} the
  deployment and Python pathways in
  \href{port05-optional-pathways.qmd}{port05}.
\end{itemize}

Tags like \texttt{{[}required{]}} and \texttt{{[}demo-only{]}} are
defined in \hyperref[how-to-read-submission-lists]{How To Read
Submission Lists}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Objectives}\label{objectives-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Objectives}

\markright{Objectives}

By the end of the week you can:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  run a project in a fresh Codespace using only documented instructions;
\item
  log failures before repairing them;
\item
  improve README, path, dependency, citation, and output hygiene;
\item
  use M4 peer review to find correctness, reproducibility, auditability,
  and KT risks;
\item
  decide whether optional deployment pathways are worth pursuing after
  the core portfolio works.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-10}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 10 produced a report and M3 project update. Week 11 tests whether
that work can be reviewed in a fresh environment. The narrated
presentation deck and final portfolio revisions then turn that evidence
into the submitted portfolio.

\chapter*{Surgery Goals}\label{surgery-goals}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Surgery Goals}

\markboth{Surgery Goals}{Surgery Goals}

\section*{Where This Fits}\label{where-this-fits-23}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where This Fits}

\markright{Where This Fits}

Portfolio surgery is the week when you stop asking, ``What else can we
add?'' and start asking, ``What would fail for a reviewer?''

Portfolio surgery means revising existing work so it is reproducible,
readable, professional, privacy-safe, and understandable to someone
outside the course. In practice, that is four kinds of work: fixing what
is broken, simplifying what is confusing, documenting what is
undocumented, and polishing what is rough.

The strongest final projects are not the ones with the most tools. They
are the ones another person can open, rerun, inspect, and understand.

\section*{The Core Standard}\label{the-core-standard}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Core Standard}

\markright{The Core Standard}

The surgery covers all of it --- the analysis code, the report, the
dashboard-style KT product, the README, and the repository itself. You
are not revising just one piece; you are revising everything a reviewer
will touch.

By the end of this week, the group should know:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  whether the report renders;
\item
  whether the dashboard-style KT product opens or runs;
\item
  whether paths are relative;
\item
  whether data access is documented;
\item
  whether citations resolve;
\item
  whether AI-use notes are specific;
\item
  whether README instructions match reality.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Do Not Add New Work Unless It Reduces
Risk}\label{do-not-add-new-work-unless-it-reduces-risk}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Do Not Add New Work Unless It Reduces
Risk}

\markright{Do Not Add New Work Unless It Reduces Risk}

Good Week 11 changes:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  fix a broken path;
\item
  add a missing package note;
\item
  rewrite unclear README steps;
\item
  regenerate stale outputs;
\item
  document a known limitation;
\item
  remove an unsupported claim.
\end{itemize}

Risky Week 11 changes:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  replacing the dashboard pathway;
\item
  adding a new package without documenting it;
\item
  adding a new analysis that is not needed for the report;
\item
  switching to public publishing without privacy review.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Group Artifact Mindset}\label{group-artifact-mindset}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Group Artifact Mindset}

\markright{Group Artifact Mindset}

M4 and the final portfolio are group project artifacts unless a brief
explicitly says otherwise. Divide roles during testing, but keep the
repository evidence in one shared place.

\section*{Check Yourself}\label{check-yourself-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Check Yourself}

\markright{Check Yourself}

At the end of the week, a reviewer should be able to say:

\begin{quote}
I can find the report, open the KT product, trace the data source,
understand the limitation, and see what still might fail.
\end{quote}

\chapter*{Fresh Codespace Test}\label{fresh-codespace-test}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Fresh Codespace Test}

\markboth{Fresh Codespace Test}{Fresh Codespace Test}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-11}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Test whether a project can run in a fresh environment and document the
results and any failures before final submission.

This page is the main Week 11 runbook. Use it for the Portfolio Surgery
session, M4 peer review, and final portfolio preparation.

\section*{Roles}\label{roles}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Roles}

\markright{Roles}

For group testing, assign roles:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Driver:} runs the project exactly as documented.
\item
  \textbf{Navigator:} reads instructions aloud and watches for
  ambiguity.
\item
  \textbf{Auditor:} logs paths, dependencies, citations, data
  provenance, and AI-use issues.
\end{itemize}

Rotate after the first major failure or after 20 minutes.

\section*{Runbook}\label{runbook}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Runbook}

\markright{Runbook}

Complete these steps in a new Codespace or equivalent clean environment.
Work from the repository root unless the project README says otherwise.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open the repository from GitHub.
\item
  Record the latest commit hash.
\item
  Read the README before running code.
\item
  Follow the documented setup steps only.
\item
  Restore or install dependencies using documented instructions.
\item
  Render the final report or book.
\item
  Open or run the dashboard-style KT product.
\item
  Check that data paths are relative.
\item
  Check that citations resolve.
\item
  Check that figures and tables are generated from code.
\item
  Check that AI-use notes are present and specific.
\item
  Record the check results and any failure, warning, unclear
  instruction, or repair.
\item
  Commit any repairs with clear messages.
\item
  Submit the updated repository link and commit hash on Canvas.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Failure Log}\label{failure-log}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Failure Log}

\markright{Failure Log}

Use this table for the Portfolio Surgery session and for peer review.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Check & Result & Evidence & Fix made & Owner \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Repository opens in fresh environment & & & & \\
Packages install or load & & & & \\
Report renders & & & & \\
Dashboard or KT product opens & & & & \\
Data paths are relative & & & & \\
Citations resolve & & & & \\
README instructions are accurate & & & & \\
AI-use note is complete & & & & \\
\end{longtable}

If every check passes, keep the evidence for each check and state that
no failures were identified and no repairs were required. You will not
lose marks for having no failures to repair. Do not create an artificial
failure or make an unnecessary change.

\section*{Example Failure Log}\label{example-failure-log}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Example Failure Log}

\markright{Example Failure Log}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Check
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Result
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Evidence
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Fix made
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Owner
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Repository opens in fresh environment & Pass & Codespace opened from
GitHub & None & Group \\
Packages install or load & Needs repair & \texttt{library(tidyverse)}
failed & Added dependency note to README & Analyst \\
Report renders & Pass & \texttt{report.html} created & None & Writer \\

Replaced absolute path with relative path & Developer \\
Citations resolve & Pass & reference list appears & None & Writer \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{NHANES Exemplar Checks}\label{nhanes-exemplar-checks}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{NHANES Exemplar Checks}

\markright{NHANES Exemplar Checks}

For the NHANES case study, the checks are:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  load \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv};
\item
  render the Week 10 report template;
\item
  confirm the README explains cached data and optional CDC retrieval;
\item
  if using the Shiny exemplar as a demo, launch it from the repository
  root;
\item
  if using the static dashboard demo, open
  \texttt{weeks/week11-portfolio-surgery/static-dashboard-ojs.qmd}.
\end{itemize}

The CDC rebuild and static dashboard are optional/demo pathways, not
required portfolio work.

\section*{Optional Python Pathway}\label{optional-python-pathway}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Optional Python Pathway}

\markright{Optional Python Pathway}

If the project uses Python, add a short dependency note with:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  how Python is launched;
\item
  required packages;
\item
  whether a notebook, script, or Quarto Python chunk is the primary
  workflow;
\item
  how outputs are regenerated.
\end{itemize}

Keep the Python pathway documented, but do not add it unless it supports
the core audience-facing product.

\section*{Final Evidence and Repair
Checklist}\label{final-evidence-and-repair-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Final Evidence and Repair Checklist}

\markright{Final Evidence and Repair Checklist}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item[$\square$]
  Required outputs render or run from a fresh environment.
\item[$\square$]
  README instructions match the tested workflow.
\item[$\square$]
  Data paths are relative.
\item[$\square$]
  Data access instructions are present.
\item[$\square$]
  Citations resolve.
\item[$\square$]
  AI-use notes identify what was drafted and how it was checked.
\item[$\square$]
  Remaining risks are documented in the failure log.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{README and Repo Hygiene}\label{readme-and-repo-hygiene}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{README and Repo Hygiene}

\markboth{README and Repo Hygiene}{README and Repo Hygiene}

\section*{Why This Matters}\label{why-this-matters-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why This Matters}

\markright{Why This Matters}

A reviewer should not need private instructions to understand the
project. The README and file structure are part of the scientific
record.

Assume the reader has not taken this course. Your README and portfolio
page should explain the project purpose, data source, main result,
limitations, and how to reproduce the work.

Good repo hygiene reduces grading friction and makes the final portfolio
easier to trust.

\section*{README Minimums}\label{readme-minimums}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{README Minimums}

\markright{README Minimums}

The project README should name:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  project title and audience;
\item
  project question;
\item
  data source and access route;
\item
  folder structure;
\item
  how to render the report;
\item
  how to open or run the dashboard-style KT product;
\item
  package or environment setup;
\item
  known limitations or risks;
\item
  public/private sharing status.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Weak vs Strong: A README
Example}\label{weak-vs-strong-a-readme-example}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Weak vs Strong: A README Example}

\markright{Weak vs Strong: A README Example}

A weak README tells the reader almost nothing:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{\# Our Project}
\NormalTok{Class project for SPPH 381H. Run the code to see the results.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

A reviewer who opens this repository does not know what the project
asks, where the data comes from, or which file to run.

A strong README answers those questions directly:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{\# Sleep and Blood Pressure in US Adults (NHANES 2017{-}2018)}

\NormalTok{Audience: community health staff planning sleep{-}screening outreach.}
\NormalTok{Question: Is short sleep associated with higher blood pressure?}
\NormalTok{Data: NHANES 2017{-}2018 public files; see data/README.md for the access route.}
\NormalTok{Main result: adults reporting under 6 hours of sleep had higher average}
\NormalTok{  systolic blood pressure; see reports/final{-}report.html.}
\NormalTok{Reproduce: open reports/final{-}report.qmd and render it}
\NormalTok{  (Command Palette \textgreater{} "Quarto: Render Document").}
\NormalTok{KT product: open dashboard/index.qmd and render it the same way.}
\NormalTok{Limitations: cross{-}sectional data; no causal claims.}
\NormalTok{Sharing: private repository; course staff have access.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The difference is not length. The strong README answers the reviewer's
questions before they are asked: what the project is, who it is for,
where the data comes from, what was found, and how to rerun it.

\section*{Path Hygiene}\label{path-hygiene}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Path Hygiene}

\markright{Path Hygiene}

Use relative paths. Avoid:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{C:\textbackslash{}Users\textbackslash{}...}
\NormalTok{E:\textbackslash{}GitHub\textbackslash{}...}
\NormalTok{/Users/name/...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Prefer paths that work from the repository root, such as:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{data/clean/analysis.csv}
\NormalTok{reports/final{-}report.qmd}
\NormalTok{dashboard/index.qmd}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Dependency Notes}\label{dependency-notes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Dependency Notes}

\markright{Dependency Notes}

If the project uses R packages beyond the course defaults, document
them. If the project uses Python as an optional extension, document how
it is launched and which packages are needed.

Keep the dependency note short. The goal is enough information for a
reviewer to rerun the work.

\section*{Output Hygiene}\label{output-hygiene}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Output Hygiene}

\markright{Output Hygiene}

Generated outputs should be traceable to source code. For each important
output, ask:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Which source file generates it?
\item
  Which data file does it use?
\item
  Can it be regenerated in a fresh Codespace?
\item
  Is it safe to share publicly?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Git Hygiene}\label{git-hygiene}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Git Hygiene}

\markright{Git Hygiene}

Before final submission:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  commit repairs with meaningful messages;
\item
  avoid committing credentials, tokens, or local cache files;
\item
  keep \texttt{.gitignore} aligned with the project;
\item
  sync the final commit before submitting the repository link.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Quick Repair Pattern}\label{quick-repair-pattern}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick Repair Pattern}

\markright{Quick Repair Pattern}

Use this structure in \texttt{repair-summary.md}:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
File changed
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Problem fixed
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
How verified
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{README.md} & Missing render command & Followed command in fresh
Codespace \\
\texttt{report.qmd} & Absolute data path & Rendered with relative
path \\
\texttt{references.bib} & Missing citation & Citation appeared in
rendered report \\
\end{longtable}

\chapter*{Peer Review and M4}\label{peer-review-and-m4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Peer Review and M4}

\markboth{Peer Review and M4}{Peer Review and M4}

\section*{Where This Fits}\label{where-this-fits-24}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where This Fits}

\markright{Where This Fits}

M4 is the structured peer review checkpoint. Treat it as a group project
artifact unless your course instructions explicitly say otherwise.

The goal is actionable feedback: comments specific enough that the
receiving group can improve the final portfolio without guessing.

\section*{What to Review}\label{what-to-review}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What to Review}

\markright{What to Review}

Use four lenses:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Lens & Question \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Correctness & Do the claims match the code output and evidence? \\
Reproducibility & Can the report and KT product run or render? \\
Auditability & Are data source, AI use, and limitations visible? \\
KT value & Does the product fit the intended audience? \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Review Sequence}\label{review-sequence}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Review Sequence}

\markright{Review Sequence}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open the repository.
\item
  Read the README first.
\item
  Try the documented render or run path.
\item
  Inspect the report, dashboard-style product, and data/source notes.
\item
  Complete the M4 files listed in
  \href{../../milestones/m4-peer-review/README.md}{M4: Peer Review}.
\item
  Name one blocking risk or state why no blocking risk was found.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Useful Feedback}\label{useful-feedback}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Useful Feedback}

\markright{Useful Feedback}

Helpful:

\begin{quote}
The report renders, but the dashboard path in the README points to an
old folder. Update the README and rerun the dashboard test before final
submission.
\end{quote}

Less helpful:

\begin{quote}
Good project.
\end{quote}

\section*{M4 Files}\label{m4-files}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{M4 Files}

\markright{M4 Files}

M4 uses \texttt{milestones/m4-peer-review/submission/}.

The required files are:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{peer-review.md}
\item
  \texttt{reproducibility-check.md}
\item
  \texttt{auditability-check.md}
\item
  \texttt{kt-feedback.md}
\item
  \texttt{ai-use-note.md}
\end{itemize}

Do not invent extra files unless your group needs them for organization.

At the end of \texttt{peer-review.md}, add the three short reflection
prompts from the M4 brief: what the review changed in your own thinking,
one limitation/bias/ stewardship issue that deserves attention, and one
review habit you will apply to your own project. No separate reflection
file is submitted.

\section*{AI Use in Peer Review}\label{ai-use-in-peer-review}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{AI Use in Peer Review}

\markright{AI Use in Peer Review}

AI may help organize feedback or identify unclear prose. It must not
invent evidence. Reviewers still need to inspect the repository,
rendered output, citations, and limitations themselves.

\section*{Before Sending Feedback}\label{before-sending-feedback}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Before Sending Feedback}

\markright{Before Sending Feedback}

Check that your comments are:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  specific;
\item
  respectful;
\item
  tied to evidence;
\item
  prioritized;
\item
  useful before final submission.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Optional Pathways}\label{optional-pathways}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Optional Pathways}

\markboth{Optional Pathways}{Optional Pathways}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Demo only --- not graded}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

This pathway is a \textbf{demonstration only}. It is not graded and not
required. Using it for graded work requires \textbf{instructor approval
first}. The supported, graded pathway in this course is the Quarto
workflow.

\end{tcolorbox}

The advanced deployment and Python-flavoured pathways are optional
demonstrations. Complete the required portfolio polish first, then
explore optional pathways only if you have time and instructor approval.

Everything on this page is {[}optional --- include only if relevant to
your project{]}. Skipping it carries no penalty (tag vocabulary:
\hyperref[how-to-read-submission-lists]{How To Read Submission Lists}).

\section*{Keep the Core First}\label{keep-the-core-first}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Keep the Core First}

\markright{Keep the Core First}

Optional pathways are only useful after the core portfolio works:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  report renders;
\item
  dashboard-style KT product opens or runs;
\item
  README explains the workflow;
\item
  data source and limitations are clear;
\item
  fresh environment test has been attempted.
\end{itemize}

Do not switch tools in Week 11 unless the change reduces risk or the
instructor has approved the pathway.

\section*{Optional Shiny}\label{optional-shiny}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Optional Shiny}

\markright{Optional Shiny}

Shiny can support richer interactivity, but it introduces extra testing
and deployment questions. Use it only when the audience need justifies a
running app and the group can document how to launch it.

For this course, the NHANES Shiny app is an exemplar and demonstration.
It is not the default required pathway for every project.

\section*{Optional Static Dashboard}\label{optional-static-dashboard}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Optional Static Dashboard}

\markright{Optional Static Dashboard}

The source file
\href{https://github.com/ehsanx/HDSx/blob/main/weeks/week11-portfolio-surgery/static-dashboard-ojs.qmd}{static-dashboard-ojs.qmd
(view on GitHub)} demonstrates a no-server dashboard that reads the
NHANES CSV snapshot in the browser. It is a \emph{source file to read or
run yourself}, not a page of this book; the instructor demonstrates the
rendered version in class. It is useful as an optional portfolio
extension after the required report and KT product are reproducible.

This file remains demo-only in this pass. Do not treat it as a portfolio
requirement.

\section*{Optional Python}\label{optional-python}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Optional Python}

\markright{Optional Python}

Python may be useful for a small extension if it supports the project
and runs in Codespaces. Keep it awareness-level unless the group has a
clear reason and a documented environment.

If Python is included, document:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  where the Python code lives;
\item
  how to run it;
\item
  which packages are required;
\item
  what outputs it creates;
\item
  how it connects to the R/Quarto core.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Public Deployment}\label{public-deployment}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Public Deployment}

\markright{Public Deployment}

Public deployment is optional and should follow the Week 10 rule:
private preview unless public-safe.

Before public sharing, check:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  no restricted data;
\item
  no credentials;
\item
  no row-level sensitive examples;
\item
  aggregate outputs only;
\item
  README explains data use and limitations.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Decision Rule}\label{decision-rule}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Decision Rule}

\markright{Decision Rule}

Use the simplest pathway that serves the audience and can be reproduced
by course staff. Optional tools are welcome only when they make the
project clearer, not when they make the project harder to review.

\chapter*{Portfolio Surgery and the Final
Portfolio}\label{portfolio-surgery-and-the-final-portfolio}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Portfolio Surgery and the Final
Portfolio}

\markboth{Portfolio Surgery and the Final Portfolio}{Portfolio Surgery
and the Final Portfolio}

\section*{Where This Fits}\label{where-this-fits-25}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where This Fits}

\markright{Where This Fits}

This page connects the Week 11
\hyperref[week-11-portfolio-surgery-1]{Portfolio Surgery} session to
\href{../../milestones/m4-peer-review/README.md}{M4 peer review} and the
\href{../../milestones/final-portfolio/README.md}{Final Portfolio}.
Portfolio Surgery is an in-class working session, not a separately
graded assignment: the artifacts you produce here feed your M4 peer
review and your Final Portfolio. It does not add new deliverables.

\section*{What To Produce in the
Session}\label{what-to-produce-in-the-session}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What To Produce in the Session}

\markright{What To Produce in the Session}

Work through these in your project repository. They are working
artifacts for M4 and the portfolio, not a separate graded submission.

\subsection*{Fresh-environment reproducibility
test}\label{fresh-environment-reproducibility-test}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Fresh-environment reproducibility
test}

Document the fresh-environment test using
\hyperref[fresh-codespace-test]{port02}. Record the latest commit hash,
environment, commands, and result.

If every reproducibility check passes, that is a good outcome: document
the checks you ran and their results, and note that no failures were
found. You are not penalised for having nothing to repair --- do not
invent a failure or make an unnecessary change just to have something to
log.

\subsection*{Failure log}\label{failure-log-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Failure log}

List any failures before repairs: warnings, broken paths, missing
packages, unclear README steps, citation issues, and stale outputs. Give
each failure evidence and a result after repair or triage.

\subsection*{Repairs}\label{repairs}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Repairs}

Name any files you changed and why, or state that no repairs were
required. This can be short.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
File changed
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Reason
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Verification
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{README.md} & Added render command & Followed command in fresh
Codespace \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection*{Peer review (feeds M4)}\label{peer-review-feeds-m4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Peer review (feeds M4)}

Record the feedback you received or gave, keeping it specific and
actionable. Your \href{../../milestones/m4-peer-review/README.md}{M4
peer review} is the graded milestone; see
\hyperref[peer-review-and-m4]{port04} for how the session feeds it.

\subsection*{Final risk list}\label{final-risk-list}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Final risk list}

Name remaining risks or state that no known risks remain. Honest risk
lists are better than hidden problems. This carries directly into the
Final Portfolio.

\subsection*{AI-use note}\label{ai-use-note}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{AI-use note}

State what AI helped with and how any repairs or wording were checked.

\section*{Final Portfolio Bridge}\label{final-portfolio-bridge}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Final Portfolio Bridge}

\markright{Final Portfolio Bridge}

The revision target is all of it --- the analysis project, the report,
the dashboard-style KT product, and the repository with its README ---
not just one piece.

The final portfolio is not a separate new analysis. It gathers the
strongest version of:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  final report source and rendered output;
\item
  dashboard-style KT product or static preview;
\item
  data provenance and stewardship documentation;
\item
  reproducibility instructions;
\item
  narrated presentation slide deck;
\item
  final AI-use audit;
\item
  clear README and repository access route.
\end{itemize}

Your dashboard-style KT product may be submitted as a static preview; a
live dashboard is not required.

Use the
\href{../../milestones/final-portfolio/final-portfolio-checklist.md}{Final
Portfolio Checklist} before submission.

Two rules make this easier:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{You may revise earlier work --- and you are encouraged to.} If
  feedback on an earlier assignment showed you a better way, fix it
  before it goes into the portfolio. The portfolio is graded on the
  strongest version of your work, not the first version you submitted.
\item
  \textbf{Required files are prescribed; the rest of the structure is
  yours.} The checklist names the files every portfolio must contain.
  Beyond those files, your group is free to organize and present the
  portfolio however serves your project best.
\end{itemize}

Not sure what ``strong'' looks like? A weak portfolio is a pile of files
the reader must decode. A strong portfolio leads with a README that
answers what the project is, who it is for, what was found, and how to
rerun it. See the side-by-side
\hyperref[weak-vs-strong-a-readme-example]{weak vs strong README
example}.

\section*{Public or Private
Repository}\label{public-or-private-repository}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Public or Private Repository}

\markright{Public or Private Repository}

Use
\href{../../milestones/final-portfolio/repository-sharing-guidance.md}{Repository
Sharing Guidance}. Private repositories are appropriate when public
sharing would create data, privacy, licensing, or stewardship risk.

Public is not better by default. Reviewable, safe, and reproducible is
the target.

\section*{Before You Finish the
Session}\label{before-you-finish-the-session}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Before You Finish the Session}

\markright{Before You Finish the Session}

Check:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  fresh environment test attempted;
\item
  any failures logged before repair, or passing checks documented;
\item
  report and KT product rerun or failure clearly explained;
\item
  README matches the tested workflow;
\item
  M4 feedback is recorded;
\item
  final risk list is honest.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Before Final Portfolio}\label{before-final-portfolio}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Before Final Portfolio}

\markright{Before Final Portfolio}

After Portfolio Surgery, focus on:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  repairing any remaining blockers;
\item
  aligning presentation slides with final report claims;
\item
  checking repository access;
\item
  confirming public/private sharing choice;
\item
  syncing the final commit before Canvas submission.
\end{itemize}

\part{Project Presentation (Online)}

\chapter*{Project Presentation (Narrated
Deck)}\label{project-presentation-narrated-deck}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Project Presentation (Narrated Deck)}

\markboth{Project Presentation (Narrated Deck)}{Project Presentation
(Narrated Deck)}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-11}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

The term-project presentation is a \textbf{narrated 5-7 slide deck
submitted online with the
\href{../../milestones/final-portfolio/README.md}{Final Portfolio}} on
Dec 11. There is no in-class presentation session. The deck is the
knowledge-translation version of your project: it should emphasize
scientific clarity, knowledge translation, reproducibility, and
auditability.

Full requirements and the 10-point rubric are in the
\href{../../milestones/m5-presentation/README.md}{Presentation brief};
this page is the how-to for recording and submitting it.

\section*{What To Record}\label{what-to-record}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What To Record}

\markright{What To Record}

Record narration over your slides so a viewer can follow the story
without you in the room. Any of these is acceptable:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a narrated \texttt{slides.html} or \texttt{slides.pdf} export;
\item
  a short screen recording that walks through the slides with
  voice-over.
\end{itemize}

Keep it to the length of a 5-7 slide talk. Aim for a clear spoken
explanation of the audience, question, data, main finding, and
limitation --- not a read-aloud of every word on the slides.

\section*{Slide Submission}\label{slide-submission}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Slide Submission}

\markright{Slide Submission}

Before the portfolio deadline, commit and sync:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{milestones/m5{-}presentation/submission/slides.qmd}
\NormalTok{milestones/m5{-}presentation/submission/slides.html}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you render to PDF instead of HTML, submit \texttt{slides.pdf}. Commit
the narrated recording alongside them. Submit the GitHub repository link
on Canvas with the latest commit hash and the slide path.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{What is a commit hash?}, bottomrule=.15mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, left=2mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacityback=0, coltitle=black]

A commit hash is the unique identifier GitHub assigns every saved
version of your work. It looks like \texttt{a3f92bc}. To find it, go to
your repository on GitHub, click \textbf{Commits}, and copy the short
code on the right. You can also run \texttt{git\ log\ -\/-oneline\ -1}
in the terminal.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Slide Checklist}\label{slide-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Slide Checklist}

\markright{Slide Checklist}

Before submitting, check that:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  slides render from source in a fresh Codespace;
\item
  fonts and figures are readable on screen;
\item
  every chart has a title, units, and limitation cue;
\item
  source and data notes are visible;
\item
  the final slide gives the audience a clear takeaway;
\item
  the narration is audible and matches what is on screen;
\item
  backup notes include data source, cohort, and AI-use details.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Portfolio Reminder}\label{portfolio-reminder}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Portfolio Reminder}

\markright{Portfolio Reminder}

The presentation is scored as the \textbf{Presentation} component (10
points) of the Final Portfolio. Use the M4 peer feedback you received to
revise the final report, dashboard-style KT product, and deck together,
then confirm the
\href{../../milestones/final-portfolio/final-portfolio-checklist.md}{Final
Portfolio Checklist} before submission.

\section*{Definition Of Done}\label{definition-of-done-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definition Of Done}

\markright{Definition Of Done}

The presentation is done when the narrated slides are committed,
rendered, and submitted with the Final Portfolio, and play without
relying on local-only files.

\part{Assignments and Milestones}

\chapter*{Assignments}\label{assignments}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Assignments}

Weekly assignments are scaffolded practice. Assignments 1-3 are
foundational gates graded Complete/Incomplete. Assignments 4-9 support
the term portfolio and use the best-4-of-6 grading policy described in
the syllabus.

\section*{Grading Logic}\label{grading-logic}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Grading Logic}

\markright{Grading Logic}

Assignments 1-3 must be completed to pass the course. They are not
numerically weighted, but they establish the workflow students need for
every later assignment.

Assignments 4-9 are scored on Canvas. Only the best 4 of these 6 scores
count toward the final grade, so students have flexibility while still
practicing consistently after the foundation weeks.

\section*{Common Reproducibility
Contract}\label{common-reproducibility-contract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Common Reproducibility Contract}

\markright{Common Reproducibility Contract}

Every assignment must:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  run or render in GitHub Codespaces;
\item
  use relative paths rather than machine-specific paths;
\item
  keep source files and every explicitly required rendered output in the
  requested submission folder and commit both;
\item
  commit and sync the final work before submitting the repository link
  on Canvas;
\item
  document packages or dependencies when adding anything beyond the
  course defaults.
\end{itemize}

Do not add global \texttt{*.html} or \texttt{*.pdf} rules to the course
workspace \texttt{.gitignore}. Use the assessment's submission list as
the authority: a listed render is committed; an unlisted practice render
does not become a deliverable.

\section*{How To Read Submission
Lists}\label{how-to-read-submission-lists}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{How To Read Submission Lists}

\markright{How To Read Submission Lists}

Assignment and milestone pages use these bracketed tags. Anything
without a tag is required.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{{[}required{]}} --- must be submitted; graded.
\item
  \textbf{{[}optional --- include only if relevant to your project{]}}
  --- never required; no penalty for skipping.
\item
  \textbf{{[}if applicable --- required only if your workflow creates
  this file{]}} --- submit it when your chosen pathway produces it.
\item
  \textbf{{[}brief --- 1-3 focused bullets or sentences are enough{]}}
  --- short is correct; do not pad.
\item
  \textbf{{[}demo-only{]}} --- instructor demonstration material; not
  graded, and using the pathway for graded work needs instructor
  approval first.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Common AI-Use Expectation}\label{common-ai-use-expectation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Common AI-Use Expectation}

\markright{Common AI-Use Expectation}

AI may help students brainstorm, debug, translate, refactor, or improve
wording. Students remain responsible for checking every code path,
column name, statistic, citation, and interpretation. When AI is used,
the submission must include an AI-use note explaining what the tool
helped with, what changed, and how the result was verified.

\subsection*{The Official AI Documentation
Rule}\label{the-official-ai-documentation-rule}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{The Official AI Documentation Rule}

Two pieces work together, and only one is graded:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{ai-use-note.md} is the graded artifact.} Every
  assignment (A2 onward) and milestone submission includes one --- even
  if you did not use AI (then it is a single sentence saying so).
\item
  \textbf{Inline \texttt{\#\ AI\ prompt:} / \texttt{\#\ Verified:}
  comments are required practice} in AI-assisted code (taught in Weeks
  3, 6, and 7). They are the working audit trail you keep while coding;
  graders read the note, not every comment --- your note summarizes
  them.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ai-use-note.md}
Template}{ai-use-note.md Template}}\label{ai-use-note.md-template}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{\texttt{ai-use-note.md} Template}

Copy this into your submission folder and fill it in:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{\# AI{-}Use Note}

\FunctionTok{\#\# Audit trail}
\CommentTok{\textless{}!{-}{-} Copy your "\# AI prompt:" / "\# Verified:" comment pairs here. {-}{-}\textgreater{}}

\FunctionTok{\#\# What AI helped with}
\CommentTok{\textless{}!{-}{-} 1{-}3 sentences: which task, which tool. {-}{-}\textgreater{}}

\FunctionTok{\#\# What I changed}
\CommentTok{\textless{}!{-}{-} 1{-}3 sentences: what you edited, fixed, or rejected. {-}{-}\textgreater{}}

\FunctionTok{\#\# How I verified the result}
\CommentTok{\textless{}!{-}{-} 1{-}3 sentences: what you checked it against (data, docs, course notes). {-}{-}\textgreater{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you did not use AI, the whole note is:
\texttt{AI\ was\ not\ used\ for\ this\ submission.}

\subsection*{Filled Example}\label{filled-example}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Filled Example}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{\# AI{-}Use Note}

\FunctionTok{\#\# Audit trail}
\FunctionTok{\# AI prompt: ggplot2 bar chart of mean BMI by income group, with labels}
\FunctionTok{\# Verified: column names against names(nhanes); compared means with summary()}

\FunctionTok{\#\# What AI helped with}
\NormalTok{Copilot drafted the initial ggplot2 code for the income{-}group plot and}
\NormalTok{suggested axis label wording.}

\FunctionTok{\#\# What I changed}
\NormalTok{The draft used a column called "income" that does not exist; I replaced it}
\NormalTok{with IncomeGroup. I also removed a misleading y{-}axis limit it added.}

\FunctionTok{\#\# How I verified the result}
\NormalTok{I re{-}ran the summary statistics without the plot and checked that the bar}
\NormalTok{heights match the group means; I confirmed the caption says the result is}
\NormalTok{descriptive and unweighted.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\chapter{Assignment 1: Modern
Workflows}\label{assignment-1-modern-workflows}

\section{Purpose}\label{purpose-3}

This required Complete/Incomplete checkpoint confirms that your personal
course workspace works before the analysis weeks begin. You will edit
the supplied root-level Quarto file, render it, commit both source and
output, sync the commit, and submit the repository link.

\section{Due Timing}\label{due-timing}

Submit by \textbf{Monday after the Week 2 class at 4:00 PM}.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Launch or reopen a Codespace from your personal workspace fork.
\item
  Edit YAML, Markdown, and a simple R code chunk.
\item
  Render a self-contained HTML file and locate it in the Explorer.
\item
  Commit and sync a reproducible change.
\item
  Verify the commit on GitHub and stop the Codespace.
\end{itemize}

\section{Inputs}\label{inputs-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Personal workspace linked from Canvas
\item
  Root starter: \texttt{practice\_report.qmd}
\item
  Synthetic practice data: \texttt{data/patients.csv}
\item
  Week 2 workflow page
\end{itemize}

\section{Tasks}\label{tasks}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Open your personal workspace fork and launch or reopen its Codespace.
\item
  At the repository root, open the supplied
  \texttt{practice\_report.qmd}.
\item
  Change the YAML \texttt{author} field to your name.
\item
  Add a \texttt{\#\#\ My\ Notes} section with 2--3 sentences about what
  you learned.
\item
  Use at least one Markdown feature such as bold text, italics, or a
  list.
\item
  Modify an existing code chunk or add one small R calculation.
\item
  Render the document and confirm \texttt{practice\_report.html} appears
  beside the source file and contains your edits and chart.
\item
  In Source Control, inspect the changes and stage both
  \texttt{practice\_report.qmd} and \texttt{practice\_report.html}.
\item
  Commit with a clear message, sync, and verify the commit on GitHub.
\item
  Stop the Codespace when finished.
\end{enumerate}

\section{AI-Use Expectations}\label{ai-use-expectations}

AI may help explain an error or Quarto syntax. If you use AI, add one
sentence under \texttt{\#\#\ My\ Notes} naming what it helped with and
how you verified the result. No separate AI-use file is required for A1.

\section{Reproducibility
Requirements}\label{reproducibility-requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Work in the supplied personal workspace rather than the read-only
  course book.
\item
  Keep \texttt{practice\_report.qmd} and \texttt{practice\_report.html}
  at the repository root.
\item
  Use only relative paths.
\item
  Commit the required rendered HTML with its source.
\end{itemize}

\section{What To Submit}\label{what-to-submit}

Submit your personal workspace repository link on Canvas. The linked,
synced commit must contain these two root-level files:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{practice\_report.qmd}
\item
  \texttt{practice\_report.html}
\end{itemize}

No reflection file, commit-evidence file, screenshot, or nested A1
submission folder is required. The repository link and Git history
provide the commit evidence.

\section{Grading Checklist}\label{grading-checklist}

Complete:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the root starter contains the required YAML, Markdown, and code edits;
\item
  \texttt{practice\_report.html} renders from that source and is
  committed;
\item
  paths are relative;
\item
  the final commit is synced and visible through the submitted link.
\end{itemize}

Needs work:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  either required root file is missing;
\item
  the rendered output does not match the source;
\item
  the code does not render or uses a machine-specific path;
\item
  the final change is not synced.
\end{itemize}

\section{Definition of Done}\label{definition-of-done-9}

A1 is done when the Canvas link opens your synced personal workspace and
the root-level source and rendered HTML show the completed workflow
check.

\chapter{Assignment 2: R With AI}\label{assignment-2-r-with-ai-2}

\section{Purpose}\label{purpose-4}

This assignment turns the Week 3 R basics into a small auditable
analysis note. You will load data with a relative path, inspect it
before summarizing, use \texttt{dplyr} verbs, write one custom function,
and document how any AI-generated code was checked.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Load R packages and import a CSV with a relative path.
\item
  Inspect rows, columns, variable types, and missingness before
  analysis.
\item
  Use \texttt{filter()}, \texttt{mutate()}, \texttt{group\_by()}, and
  \texttt{summarise()} for a descriptive summary.
\item
  Write one small custom function and test it on the dataset.
\item
  Audit AI-generated code for wrong column names, missingness mistakes,
  and overclaiming.
\end{itemize}

\section{Inputs}\label{inputs-3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Week 3 page: \texttt{weeks/week03-r-with-ai/index.qmd}
\item
  Starter note:
  \texttt{assignments/assignment02-r-with-ai/starter-analysis.qmd}
\item
  Dataset: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Submission folder:
  \texttt{assignments/assignment02-r-with-ai/submission/}
\end{itemize}

\section{Tasks}\label{tasks-1}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Create the folder
  \texttt{assignments/assignment02-r-with-ai/submission/}.
\item
  Copy the starter structure into \texttt{analysis-note.qmd}.
\item
  Load \texttt{readr}, \texttt{dplyr}, and any other package used in the
  file.
\item
  Import the NHANES CSV using a relative path from the repository root.
\item
  Inspect the dataset with row count, column names, variable types, and
  missingness.
\item
  Create one cleaned or analysis-ready table using at least three
  \texttt{dplyr} verbs.
\item
  Write one custom function, such as a function that returns mean and SD
  for a numeric variable.
\item
  Save a small output file named \texttt{output/summary\_table.csv} ---
  this is the required filename, and the starter note already writes it.
\item
  Add AI audit comments near code that was drafted, debugged, or revised
  with AI assistance.
\item
  Render the Quarto note to HTML.
\item
  Commit and sync the source, rendered output, saved output file, and
  AI-use note.
\end{enumerate}

\section{AI-Use Expectations}\label{ai-use-expectations-1}

AI may help draft code, explain an error, or suggest a function
structure. You must verify that all column names exist, missing values
are handled intentionally, outputs match the code, and interpretations
remain descriptive. Include \texttt{ai-use-note.md} even if AI was not
used.

\section{Reproducibility
Requirements}\label{reproducibility-requirements-1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The analysis must render in GitHub Codespaces.
\item
  The data path must be relative.
\item
  Output files must be created by code, not manually edited.
\item
  Load every package used in the Quarto source and list it in
  \texttt{dependency-note.md}.
\item
  The course devcontainer is the dependency baseline. Do not initialize
  \texttt{renv}. If you think an additional package is necessary, ask
  before adding it and document the approved change so the work still
  runs in a fresh Codespace.
\end{itemize}

\section{What To Submit}\label{what-to-submit-1}

Place all submission files in:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{assignments/assignment02{-}r{-}with{-}ai/submission/}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Submit these files:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{analysis-note.qmd} {[}required{]}: Quarto source with data
  import, inspection, cleaning, function, and AI audit comments.
\item
  \texttt{analysis-note.html} {[}required{]}: rendered output.
\item
  \texttt{output/summary\_table.csv} {[}required{]}: code-generated
  output. Use the required filename listed in this submission checklist
  --- do not rename it to \texttt{clean\_data.csv}.
\item
  \texttt{ai-use-note.md} {[}required{]}: what AI helped with and how
  you checked it, or a sentence saying AI was not used. Template and
  filled example: \hyperref[the-official-ai-documentation-rule]{The
  Official AI Documentation Rule}.
\item
  \texttt{dependency-note.md} {[}required{]}: packages used and whether
  any were added beyond the course environment.
\end{itemize}

\section{Submission Route}\label{submission-route}

Submit your GitHub repository link on Canvas after committing and
syncing the required files.

\section{Grading Checklist}\label{grading-checklist-1}

Complete:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  CSV is imported with a relative path.
\item
  Dataset inspection includes size, column names/types, and missingness.
\item
  \texttt{dplyr} workflow uses at least three verbs, as stated in the
  task list.
\item
  One custom function is defined and used.
\item
  Output CSV is generated by code.
\item
  AI-use note and dependency note are present.
\item
  Quarto file renders in Codespaces.
\end{itemize}

Incomplete:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Data path is absolute or machine-specific.
\item
  Code does not render.
\item
  Missingness is ignored or silently dropped.
\item
  Function is absent or unused.
\item
  Output file is missing or manually created.
\item
  AI-use or dependency note is missing.
\end{itemize}

\section{Definition of Done}\label{definition-of-done-10}

You are done when \texttt{analysis-note.qmd} renders, the output CSV is
regenerated by code, and the Canvas submission links to the synced
repository folder.

\chapter{Assignment 3: Git
Collaboration}\label{assignment-3-git-collaboration}

\section{Purpose}\label{purpose-5}

This assignment checks that you can make a small, reviewable change
using Git. You will create a branch, make a documentation-only edit,
inspect the diff, open a pull request, and connect the workflow to M0
group repository readiness.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Make meaningful commits with clear messages.
\item
  Read a diff before committing.
\item
  Use \texttt{.gitignore} to keep generated or sensitive files out of
  version control.
\item
  Create a branch and open a pull request.
\item
  Document a limited change so collaborators can review it.
\end{itemize}

\section{Inputs}\label{inputs-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Week 4 page: \texttt{weeks/week04-git-collaboration/index.qmd}
\item
  M0 connection: \texttt{milestones/m0-group-formation/README.md}
\item
  Submission folder: \texttt{assignments/assignment03-git/submission/}
\end{itemize}

\section{Tasks}\label{tasks-2}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Create the folder \texttt{assignments/assignment03-git/submission/}.
\item
  Create a new branch with a short name such as
  \texttt{docs/git-audit-note}.
\item
  Make one documentation-only edit in your repository. Good choices
  include clarifying a README sentence, adding a path note, or improving
  a caption.
\item
  Inspect the diff and confirm the change is limited to documentation or
  text.
\item
  Check \texttt{.gitignore} and add a safe pattern if a generated or
  temporary file is being tracked.
\item
  Commit with a message that explains the change.
\item
  Push the branch and open a pull request.
\item
  If a pull request cannot be opened, do not stop: take a screenshot of
  your branch and diff, and write a short explanation instead. Both go
  in \texttt{pull-request-evidence.md}.
\item
  In the pull request description, explain what changed and how you
  verified the scope.
\item
  Add a short note on whether your group repository is ready for M0:
  members, access, communication plan, and Codespaces access.
\end{enumerate}

\section{AI-Use Expectations}\label{ai-use-expectations-2}

AI may help summarize a diff or draft a pull request description. You
must verify the diff yourself and make sure the pull request does not
include unrelated file changes. Include an AI-use sentence in
\texttt{git-audit-note.md}.

Save the evidence as you work: copy the AI prompt or output you used
into \texttt{git-audit-note.md} (for example: ``I used AI to summarize
the diff; I re-read the diff myself and confirmed the summary is
accurate.''), and paste the pull request link plus one explanatory line
into \texttt{pull-request-evidence.md} (for example: ``PR link: \ldots{}
--- a documentation-only edit to one README sentence.''). Fuller worked
examples of both files are on the Week 4 \hyperref[ai-auditing]{AI
Auditing page}.

\section{Reproducibility
Requirements}\label{reproducibility-requirements-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  All changes must be visible in Git history.
\item
  The pull request must show a focused diff.
\item
  \texttt{.gitignore} must not hide source files needed to reproduce the
  work.
\item
  Evidence files must use relative paths or GitHub URLs.
\end{itemize}

\section{What To Submit}\label{what-to-submit-2}

Place all submission files in:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{assignments/assignment03{-}git/submission/}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Submit these files:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{pull-request-evidence.md} {[}required{]}: pull request URL,
  branch name, and final commit hash. If a pull request cannot be
  opened, include screenshot filenames and a written explanation.
\item
  \texttt{documentation-change.md} {[}required{]}: the before/after text
  or a link to the changed documentation file.
\item
  \texttt{git-audit-note.md} {[}required{]}: diff summary,
  \texttt{.gitignore} check, AI-use sentence, and M0 readiness note.
\end{itemize}

\section{Submission Route}\label{submission-route-1}

Submit your GitHub repository link on Canvas after committing and
syncing the required files.

\section{Grading Checklist}\label{grading-checklist-2}

Complete:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Branch was created for the work.
\item
  Documentation-only change is focused and reviewable.
\item
  Diff was inspected and summarized.
\item
  Commit message is clear.
\item
  Pull request evidence or equivalent text evidence is included.
\item
  \texttt{.gitignore} was checked.
\item
  M0 readiness note names repository access and team communication
  status.
\end{itemize}

Incomplete:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Work was committed directly without evidence of a branch or review
  step.
\item
  Diff includes unrelated changes.
\item
  Pull request evidence is missing.
\item
  \texttt{.gitignore} check is absent.
\item
  M0 readiness note is missing.
\item
  Work is not synced to GitHub.
\end{itemize}

\section{Definition of Done}\label{definition-of-done-11}

You are done when the Canvas submission links to a synced repository
with pull request evidence, a documentation change summary, and a Git
audit note in the Assignment 3 submission folder.

\chapter{Assignment 4: Polyglot
Parity}\label{assignment-4-polyglot-parity}

\section{Purpose}\label{purpose-6}

This assignment strengthens R first, then translates a small descriptive
task to Python/pandas and checks whether both languages produce the same
answer. The goal is careful reading of code in both R and Python, not
Python mastery.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-5}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Build a trusted R \texttt{dplyr} summary before translating it.
\item
  Refactor repeated R logic into a small function.
\item
  Translate a simple summary to Python/pandas with AI support if useful.
\item
  Run parity checks for row counts, group labels, and rounded
  statistics.
\item
  Keep notebook outputs and dependency notes clean for review.
\end{itemize}

\section{Inputs}\label{inputs-5}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Week 5 page: \texttt{weeks/week05-polyglot-r-deepening/index.qmd}
\item
  R deepening page:
  \texttt{weeks/week05-polyglot-r-deepening/r-deepening-parity.qmd}
\item
  Dataset: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Parity template:
  \texttt{assignments/assignment04-polyglot/parity-table-template.csv}
\item
  Submission folder:
  \texttt{assignments/assignment04-polyglot/submission/}
\end{itemize}

\section{Tasks}\label{tasks-3}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Create or use the folder
  \texttt{assignments/assignment04-polyglot/submission/}.
\item
  Create \texttt{polyglot-parity.qmd}.
\item
  In R, load the NHANES CSV with a relative path.
\item
  Produce a descriptive BMI summary by \texttt{IncomeGroup} and
  \texttt{Gender}.
\item
  Refactor the R summary into one small reusable function.
\item
  Create \texttt{translation.ipynb} and translate the same summary to
  Python/pandas.
\item
  Create \texttt{helpers.py} with at least one reusable Python helper
  called from the notebook.
\item
  Complete a parity table comparing row counts, filtered row counts,
  grouping labels, and rounded summary values.
\item
  Write a dependency note naming the R and Python packages used. If an
  instructor-approved Python package is added, also update the root
  \texttt{.devcontainer/requirements.txt}; do not create a second
  manifest in the submission folder.
\item
  Clear bulky notebook outputs before committing.
\item
  Render the Quarto file to HTML.
\item
  Commit and sync the assignment folder.
\end{enumerate}

\section{AI-Use Expectations}\label{ai-use-expectations-3}

AI may help translate R to Python, explain tracebacks, or suggest helper
functions. You must verify that the translated code answers the same
question, uses the same filters, handles missingness the same way, and
returns matching grouped results after rounding. Include
\texttt{ai-use-note.md}.

\section{Reproducibility
Requirements}\label{reproducibility-requirements-3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  R and Python files must run in GitHub Codespaces.
\item
  Paths must be relative to the repository.
\item
  The notebook must not contain large stale outputs.
\item
  Dependencies must be documented in \texttt{dependency-note.md}.
\item
  Differences between R and Python results must be explained before
  changing code.
\end{itemize}

\section{What To Submit}\label{what-to-submit-3}

Place all submission files in:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{assignments/assignment04{-}polyglot/submission/}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Submit these files:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{polyglot-parity.qmd}: R analysis, R function, parity
  discussion, and rendered-output instructions.
\item
  \texttt{polyglot-parity.html}: rendered Quarto output.
\item
  \texttt{translation.ipynb}: Python/pandas translation notebook.
\item
  \texttt{helpers.py}: reusable Python helper script called from the
  notebook.
\item
  \texttt{parity-table.csv}: completed parity table.
\item
  \texttt{dependency-note.md}: Python/R dependency note.
\item
  \texttt{ai-use-note.md}: what AI helped translate or debug and how you
  verified the result.
\end{itemize}

\section{Submission Route}\label{submission-route-2}

Submit your GitHub repository link on Canvas after committing and
syncing the required files.

\section{Grading Checklist}\label{grading-checklist-3}

Canvas applies a 7-point rubric. This assignment is part of the
best-4-of-6 set for Assignments 4-9:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Criterion & Points \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
R summary is correct, clear, and uses a relative data path & 1 \\
R function reproduces the summary logic & 1 \\
Python translation matches the same analytic question & 1 \\
Parity table checks row counts, groups, and rounded values & 1.5 \\
Dependency and notebook hygiene are documented & 1 \\
AI-use note explains generation, changes, and verification & 1 \\
Files render/run from the submission folder and are synced & 0.5 \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Definition of Done}\label{definition-of-done-12}

You are done when the R summary, Python translation, and parity table
agree or any differences are clearly explained, and the required files
are committed in the Assignment 4 submission folder.

\chapter{Assignment 5: Visualization
Audit}\label{assignment-5-visualization-audit}

\section{Purpose}\label{purpose-7}

This assignment turns a descriptive summary into an honest visual
explanation. You will create a \texttt{ggplot2} visualization, audit a
flawed plot, revise your own design, and write a caption that states the
statistic, data source, exclusions, and limitations.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-6}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Create a clear \texttt{ggplot2} visualization from the NHANES
  classroom dataset.
\item
  Identify misleading scale, title, grouping, missingness, and caption
  choices.
\item
  Revise a plot so the visual design matches the evidence.
\item
  Explain the descriptive and unweighted limits of the figure.
\item
  Connect visualization readiness to the M1 project proposal.
\end{itemize}

\section{Inputs}\label{inputs-6}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Dataset: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Worked example:
  \texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/viz03-worked-example.qmd}
\item
  Studio activity:
  \texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/viz06-studio-visual-audit.qmd}
\item
  Walkthrough and reference:
  \texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/viz07-assignment-a5-and-reference.qmd}
\item
  Flawed plot starter in your personal workspace:
  \texttt{assignments/assignment05-visualization/flawed-plot-code.R}
\item
  Visual-audit template in your personal workspace:
  \texttt{assignments/assignment05-visualization/visual-audit-note-template.md}
\item
  M1 connection: \texttt{milestones/m1-proposal/README.md}
\end{itemize}

\section{Tasks}\label{tasks-4}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Create or use the folder
  \texttt{assignments/assignment05-visualization/submission/}.
\item
  Review the Week 6 worked example.
\item
  Choose one descriptive comparison from the NHANES CSV snapshot.
\item
  Draft an initial plot using R and \texttt{ggplot2}.
\item
  Audit the flawed plot starter and your own draft for scale choices,
  labels, missing context, aggregation, and overclaiming.
\item
  Revise your plot so it supports a descriptive interpretation.
\item
  Export the corrected plot as \texttt{corrected-plot.png}.
\item
  Write a caption that includes data source, statistic, exclusions, and
  limitation.
\item
  Add a \texttt{plotly} preview {[}optional --- include only if relevant
  to your project{]}: only if it helps you inspect the figure; the
  required submission is still the static plot.
\item
  Write a short M1 link note naming how the visualization standard
  affects your project dataset, audience, or feasibility.
\end{enumerate}

\section{AI-Use Expectations}\label{ai-use-expectations-4}

AI may help draft \texttt{ggplot2} code, suggest labels, or identify
possible visual problems. You must verify the code against real column
names, check that the scale does not exaggerate the result, and remove
causal or population-level claims that the plot cannot support. Include
\texttt{ai-use-note.md}.

\section{Reproducibility
Requirements}\label{reproducibility-requirements-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The Quarto file must render in GitHub Codespaces.
\item
  The dataset path must be relative.
\item
  The plot image must be exported by code or a documented render step.
\item
  Captions must state that the analysis is descriptive and unweighted
  when using the classroom NHANES snapshot.
\end{itemize}

\section{What To Submit}\label{what-to-submit-4}

Place all submission files in:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{assignments/assignment05{-}visualization/submission/}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Submit these files:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{visualization-audit.qmd} {[}required{]}: rerunnable Quarto
  file that loads the CSV, creates the final plot, and explains the
  revision.
\item
  \texttt{visualization-audit.html} {[}required{]}: rendered output.
\item
  \texttt{corrected-plot.png} {[}required{]}: exported final plot.
\item
  \texttt{visual-audit-note.md} {[}required{]}: at least three issues
  checked or corrected.
\item
  \texttt{caption.md} {[}required{]} {[}brief --- 1-3 focused bullets or
  sentences are enough{]}: final figure caption.
\item
  \texttt{m1-link-note.md} {[}required{]} {[}brief --- 1-3 focused
  bullets or sentences are enough{]}: two or three sentences connecting
  the visualization standard to M1.
\item
  \texttt{ai-use-note.md} {[}required{]}: what AI helped with and how
  you checked it, or a sentence saying AI was not used.
\end{itemize}

\section{Submission Route}\label{submission-route-3}

Submit your GitHub repository link on Canvas after committing and
syncing the required files.

Canvas applies a 7-point rubric. This assignment is part of the
best-4-of-6 set for Assignments 4-9:
